The Applicants ought not at this late stage be allowed to change fundamentally the nature of their objection to now indicate dissatisfaction with a particular, namely, value of opening stock on 1 July 1995. The Applicants have had ample opportunity, as set out above, and have eschewed the opportunity to amend their objections to raise it as an alternative ground, notwithstanding the Commissioner's invitation so to do on 18 June 2002.
  2. Section 14ZZK(a) of the TAA provides that in a review by the Tribunal "the Applicant is, unless the Tribunal orders otherwise, limited to the grounds stated in the taxation objection to which the decision relates". Paragraph 1(b) of the Applicants' objection is specific. The value of trading stock on hand at the beginning of the 1996 year disclosed in the return is correct. Paragraph 1(a) of the objection stating the amount included in income should be some lesser figure than $52,934 does not state fully and in detail the grounds relied upon: sec 14ZU of the TAA.
  3. If that ground had been raised at an appropriate time in, for example, the objections lodged on 11 January 1999 then, it is highly likely the Commissioner may have proceeded to amend the 1995 and earlier years of income, subject to limitation periods in section 170 of the 1936 Act.
  4. If the issue and contention had been raised in the notice of contention or, for that matter, in opening address then the Commissioner would have presented a different case to the Tribunal.
  5. Thus, for example certified copies of the notices of assessment under section 177 of the 1936 Act for the years of income from 1991 to 1995 inclusive may have been tendered to the Tribunal. Evidence may have been led as to whether the Applicants, or any of them, objected to their assessments in any of the years of income from 1991 to 1995 in respect of the particular, namely, the value of opening and closing stock in each year of income.
  6. Evidence may also have been led by the Commissioner as to the precise date upon which the Commissioner became aware that the closing stock figure at 30 June 1995 contained write downs in respect of Aged Stock.
  7. In particular expert accounting evidence would have been lead from Mr. McClintock as to the appropriateness of having a difference in amount between the value of closing stock for one year and opening stock for the next year. That is matter on which expert accounting evidence could and should have been adduced.
  8. Because the Applicants did not raise the issue until the written submissions dated 30 April 2002, and because they did not properly make application for an amendment to their objections, the Commissioner is prejudiced because he would have run his case differently, and because he is denied the opportunity to adduce expert evidence directly on point.
  9. If the Applicants are permitted to take the point, the Commissioner submits in the alternative that it is devoid of merit on the grounds set out above in paragraph 145 and following, below.

Legislative history

  1. The issue in dispute concerns the treatment of the opening value of trading stock for the purposes of section 29 of the 1936 Act. The section provides:

"The value of live stock and of each article of other trading stock to be taken into account at the beginning of the year of income shall be its value as ascertained under this or the previous Act at the end of the year immediately preceding the year of income."

  1. The initial trading stock provision was paragraph 14(a) of the Income Tax Assessment Act 1915-1921. It provided:
    1. The income of any person shall include –

(a) profits derived from any trade or business and converted into stock-in-trade or added to the capital of or in any way invested in the trade or business:

Provided that for the purpose of computing such profits the value of all live-stock, produce, goods and merchandise (not being plant used in the production of income) not disposed of at the beginning and end of the year in which the income was derived shall be taken into account."

  1. That form of wording from sec 14 of the 1915 Act was carried over to the Income Tax Assessment Act 1922. Section 16(a) of that Act provided:
    1. The assessable income of any person shall include –

(a) profits derived from any trade or business and converted into stock-in-trade or added to the capital of or in any way invested in the trade or business:

Provided that for the purpose of computing such profits the value of all live-stock (not being live-stock used as beasts of burden or as working beasts), and trading stock (not being live-stock) not disposed of at the beginning and end of the period in which the income was derived shall be taken into account:

For the purposes of this paragraph "Value" means-

(i) in the case of live-stock (not being live-stock used as beasts of burden or as working beasts) – the value as prescribed; and

(ii) in the case of trading stock (not being live-stock) - the actual cost price or market selling value of each article of trading stock, or the price at which each article of trading stock can be replaced, at the option of the taxpayer in respect of each article."

  1. At this point there existed a trading stock adjustment provision but there was no equivalent to sec 29 requiring continuity from one income year to the next year. In 1923 amendments were introduced to allow primary producers the option of valuing the natural increase of livestock, at cost price as specified or market selling price. In 1924 amendments were introduced to deal specifically with the question whether the option to value livestock, once exercised, was irrevocable. For the first time the precursor to section 29 appeared in the Act.
  2. The amended sec 16(a) of the 1922 Act provided:

16. The assessable income of any person shall include –

(a) profits derived from any trade or business and converted into stock-in-trade or added to the capital of or in any way invested in the trade or business:

Provided that for the purpose of computing such profits the value of all live-stock (not being live-stock used as beasts of burden or as working beasts), and trading stock (not being live-stock) not disposed of at the beginning and end of the period in which the income was derived shall be taken into account except where the taxpayer otherwise elects, as provided in paragraph (aa) of this section:

For the purposes of this paragraph "Value" means-

(i) in the case of trading stock (not being live-stock) - the actual cost price or market selling value of each article of trading stock, or the price at which each article of trading stock can be replaced, at the option of the taxpayer in respect of each article;

Provided that the value adopted in relation to any article of trading stock as the value of that article as at the end of the period in which the income was derived, shall, for the purposes of the assessment of the person's income derived in the next succeeding period, be deemed to be the value of that article as at the commencement of the next succeeding period, and

(ii) in the case of live-stock (not being live stock used beasts of burden or as working beasts) - the cost price or market selling price at the option of the person which shall be exercised by notice in writing signed by him and delivered by him at the office of the Commissioner on or before the prescribed date. The cost price in relation to natural increase of live stock shall be the value per head of the live stock selected by the person within the limits prescribed and the value so selected shall be used for the purposes of the assessment of the financial year beginning on the first day of July One thousand nine hundred and twenty three and of all subsequent years:

Provided that the value adopted in relation to any live stock, as the value of that live stock, as at the end of the period in which the income was derived, shall, for the purposes of the assessment of the person's income derived in the next succeeding period, be deemed to be the value of that live stock as at the commencement of the next succeeding period:

Provided also that any option exercised in pursuance of this sub-paragraph for the purposes of an assessment for the financial year beginning on the first day of July One thousand nine hundred and twenty four or any subsequent year, shall be irrevocable and shall, if the person, in the notice of his option, so requires, apply to the assessment of his income tax for the financial year beginning on the first day of July One thousand nine hundred and twenty three and shall apply to the assessment of the person's income derived in the period in respect of which the option is exercised and to assessments in respect of which the option is exercised and to assessments in respect of all subsequent periods. (Emphasis added)

  1. Section 29 of the 1936 Act was introduced as part of the harmonisation of the Commonwealth and State income tax legislation. Although it was clearly based upon paragraph 16(a) of the Act 1922-1936, it also reflects the recommendations of the 1934 Royal Commission on Taxation and the subsequent Conference of Commonwealth and State Commissioners of Taxation.
  2. The 1934 Royal Commission on Taxation discussed the valuation of live stock in its Third Report. Although the matter of the value of trading stock being brought to account at the beginning of a year of income being the same value at the end of the previous year was not discussed independently of the question of irrevocable elections, it did feature in a recommendation.
  3. Whilst the Royal Commission recognised the necessity for reasonable permanence in regard to any option that may be exercised by a grazier, it did not believe the election regarding the method of valuation for live stock should be irrevocable. Consequently the Royal Commission recommended a mechanism for varying the method of valuation.
  4. The recommendation featured at paragraph 796 in the Report. The relevant paragraph is subparagraph (2) –

"We recommend that a grazier who has made, or who may make, any election relating to the valuation of live stock (other than the election to omit natural increase) for the purpose of the Commonwealth or any State Income Tax Act be allowed to make a fresh election, subject to the following conditions:

(1) That the new election shall be subject to the approval of the Commissioner, and that all subsequent returns shall be prepared on that basis unless and until it is altered with the consent of the Commissioner;

(2) That in the year of change the value of live stock on hand at the beginning of the year must be brought into account at the value at which it was assessed by the Department as on the last day of the preceding year;

(3) That, in the absence of any material objection, the Commissioner shall favourably consider any application to make a new election if the object of the alteration is to bring the valuation of live stock into agreement for the purposes of the Commonwealth and State returns'. (Emphasis added)

  1. The Conference of Commonwealth and State Commissioners of Taxation, which discussed the recommendations of the 1934 Royal Commission, also adopted the premise that trading stock should be brought to account at the beginning of the year of income at the same figure as that given for the end of the preceding year.
  2. The following comment is made in the Report-

'The main principle expressed in this Division is that every trader, at the end of every income year, should bring in the value of his trading stock on hand, and that he shall have the option of bringing it in at its cost price, or the market selling price, or at its replacement price. In the following year trading stock should be brought in at the same figure as that given for the previous year.' (Emphasis added)

  1. The most explicit policy statement in relation to the meaning of section 29 appears in the transcript of the Second Reading Speech to the Income Tax Assessment Bill 1935, where the Treasurer made the following statement (at 2724 Hansard) –

'Under the Bill greater elasticity has been provided by giving the taxpayer the right to alter his elections, subject, however, to the Commissioner's consent....With the concession of this elasticity, however, one definite requirement of the Bill is that the closing value in the one return must always be the opening value in that subsequent return. This is essential to prevent the evasion of taxation.' (Emphasis added)

  1. The Treasurer's statement in the Second Reading Speech is entirely consistent with the intention that may be read into the recommendations of the 1934 Royal Commission and the subsequent discussion at the Commissioners' Conference. It is possible to conclude that section 29 was intended to prevent any tax evasion that may have been possible where the method of valuation of trading stock had changed.
  2. The section was clearly intended by the framers of the 1936 Act to operate so that the figure that was given as the closing value of trading stock in one year was the same figure as that given as the opening value of the trading stock in the subsequent year.
  3. The Applicants' contention that the words "its value as ascertained under this Act" mean its value as hypothetically or notionally adjusted imply an ambiguity in the construction of sec 29. Accordingly resort to the legislative history, explanatory memorandum or notes and extrinsic materials is authorized by reference to sec 15AA of the Acts Interpretation Act 1901.
  4. For completeness it should be noted the 1997 Act has effected a slight change in wording in the equivalent provision. Section 70-40 replaces section 29. The section provides –

"70-40 Value of trading stock at start of income year

(1) The Value of an item of trading stock on hand at the start of an income year is the same amount at which it was taken into account under this Division at the end of the last income year.

(2) The value of the item is a nil amount if the item was not taken into account under this Division at the end of the last income year.

  1. The wording ensures that the opening value of each item of stock is always the same as the closing value it had in the previous year. If the amount is not taken into account at the end of a year, its opening value at the start of the next year will be nil. The Explanatory Memorandum provides as follows –

'Section 29 of the 1936 Act says that an items opening value is the value ascertained under the Act at the end of the previous year. There are some Board of Review decisions concluding that those words mean that the opening value must be what the previous years closing value should have been. If the previous years assessment cannot be amended because of time limits, its closing value will be different from the next years opening value. This will produce either a windfall gain or an unexpected loss for the taxpayer.

The rewrite avoids the possible problem. If one years closing value is amended, then the next years opening value will change to reflect that amendment. If the closing value cannot be amended, the next years opening value will still be what was recorded as the closing value. Subsection 70-40(2) supports this by ensuring that an items opening value is nil if the items closing value in the previous year was not taken into account at all.' (Emphasis added)

  1. There is, in the 1997 rewrite no legislative recognition that the Board decisions are necessarily correct. All that the 1997 Act evinces is a legislative intention, as an abundance of caution in the rewrite process, to ensure there was no 'windfall gain or an unexpected loss for the taxpayer". The anomalies created by acceptance of the taxpayers contentions are discussed at paragraph 189 and following, below.

Academic Commentary

  1. Academic commentary on section 29 with the exception of the commentary in the CCH Federal Tax Reporter paragraph [17-555] is diametrically opposed to the Applicants' contentions. The CCH commentary is addressed at paragraph 219 and following, below.
  2. Thus in Ryan's 'Manual of the Law of Income Tax in Australia' 7th Ed (1989) the effect of s. 29 is simply stated as follows:

'Section 29 merely requires that the value given to stock at the beginning of a year of income agrees with the value given to it at the close of the preceding year of income'.

  1. The same approach is taken by Lehmann and Coleman 'Taxation Law in Australia' 4th Ed 1996 at paragraph 5.1490 as follows:

'The three methods prescribed by s. 31(1) are: cost price, market selling value and replacement price. Section 29 provides that the value of trading stock at the commencement of the year of income shall be the same as the value at the end of the previous year.'

  1. The same approach is adopted in Gunn's 'Commonwealth Income Law and Practice' 7th Ed 1963 where it is stated as follows:

'Value of Trading Stock at beginning of year of income

The value of all trading stock on hand at the beginning of a year of income must correspond with the value of stock on hand at end of preceding year. Ascertainment of the value of stock on hand at end of an income year is provided for in sections 31 and 32.

See Steel Barrel Co Limited v Osborne (1948) 30 TC 73, where it was said that a sum fixed as value of trading stock at close of an income year would 'of necessity also be the opening figure' for the next year (per Vasey, J at page 81).

The taxpayer, a grazier, had over a number of years omitted to disclose in his income tax returns the value of substantial quantities of wool on hand at the end of each year. After this omission was finally disclosed, the Commissioner amended the assessments for the ten years from 1948 onwards, but was debarred by s.24 of the NZ Act from amending the assessment for 1947 which had been made on the basis that there was no wool on hand at 31 March 1947. In amending the assessment for 1948, the Commissioner brought to account the value, £2505, of wool on hand at 31 March 1948, but ignored the value of wool on hand at the beginning of the year, £1842. Held: the 1947 assessment, which could not be reopened under section 24, was deemed under section 26 (cf section 177 of Commonwealth Act) to be correct in all its particulars; accordingly, both taxpayer and the Commissioner were estopped from attributing any value to the wool on hand at 31 March 1947 other than nil and by virtue of section 98(3) (cf section 29 of Commonwealth Act) this same value was to be adopted for the opening stock in the 1948 assessment (Kirkpatrick v IRC (NZ) (1962), 8 AITR 469).' (Emphasis added)

  1. This same approach is taken in Cooper Krever and Vann 'Income Taxation Commentary and Materials' 1989 at page 627 as follows:

'Changing Valuation Methods

Taxpayers are given a great deal of latitude in valuing their stock. The reference in section 31 to valuing 'each article' of stock suggests that each and every asset may be valued separately and by a different method and it appears that methods of valuation can change annually. Virtually the only obligation of consistency is across years: section 29 requires the closing value allocated to stock to be the opening value of the next year.' (Emphasis added)

  1. The same principle is contained in Woellner, Vella and Burns 'Australian Taxation Law' 4th Ed 1994 at paragraph 14-080 as follows:

'As section 31(1) refers to "each article of trading stock" it is open to the taxpayer to value some items of stock at cost, some at market selling value and some at replacement price. Further, the taxpayer is not bound to maintain a particular basis of valuation from year to year and can adopt a completely different "mix" of valuations in any subsequent year, subject only to the over-riding requirement of section 29 that the value of trading stock on hand at the beginning of a year of income must be the same value as that adopted at the end of the preceding year of income.' (Emphasis added)

  1. Put simply, the academic commentators, including in particular Gunn and Cooper Krever and Vann are emphatic. The trading stock provisions allow great flexibility. Individual items can be valued separately and by different methods. The particular method adopted for a specific individual piece of stock may change from year to year. But the fixed and immutable rule is, there can be no inconsistency across income years between closing stock in year one and opening stock in year two.

General law concepts of opening and closing stock

  1. The decision in Steel Barrel Co Ltd v Osborne (1948) 30 TC page 73 involved an agreement between the revenue authorities and the taxpayer for the 1933 to 1937 years inclusive. By that agreement the opening and closing stock figures were accordingly reduced for 1933 to 1937. By an oversight the revenue omitted to reduce the opening stock figure for the 1938 year so as to correspond with the closing figure of stock as at 31 December 1937.
  2. The case turns in part on the terms of agreement between the parties. However Vaisey J., in a passage referred to in part by Gunn's commentary, stated a matter of general principle as follows:

"It is perfectly true that the agreement in question purported to relate, and did relate in terms, to the years 1933 to 1937 inclusive, and to no other year. But the difficulty in the way of the present Appellants would appear to be this. A conventional figure having been fixed by agreement as the closing figure at the end of 1937, it would, in my judgment, of necessity also be the opening figure for the year 1938.

[The] substitution of a different figure as the opening figure for 1938 would be in itself surprising." (Emphasis added)

Anomalies: unexpected losses and windfall gains

  1. The position advocated by the Applicants, namely, that the value of opening stock on hand at the beginning of year of income may be amended irrespective of whether or not the closing stock figure for the immediately preceding year of income is amended or not, produces distortions and has the effect that either an amount falls through between income years and escapes tax completely, to the detriment of the revenue, or, double taxation occurs to the taxpayer.
  2. As set out above at paragraphs 156 it was the existence of such distortions by way of "windfall gains or unexpected losses" or the "essential to prevent the evasion of taxation" that lead the legislature to introduce sec 29 and sec 70-40 in their then existing form.
  3. The windfall gains or unexpected losses arise as to whether the closing stock figure is above or below the opening stock figure. If the value of closing stock on hand at the end of year one is lower than the opening stock figure the taxpayer is denied a deduction for its stock that it is entitled to. This is because the inflated value of closing stock on hand at the end of year one (less opening stock for that year) is added back into income as the trading stock adjustment under sec 28. Correspondingly, if the value of closing stock on hand at the end of year one is less than opening stock in year two, the amount of the increase escapes tax altogether.
  4. Two scenarios can be envisaged, namely, where the adjusted opening stock figure exceeds the unamended closing stock figure from the previous year or, alternatively, the adjusted opening stock figure is less than the unamended closing stock figure from the previous year.
  5. Suppose the cost of an article is $100, its market selling value in the taxpayers' ordinary domestic market is $160 and its market selling value in a special export market is $220. Assume, for the moment, that contrary to the decisions in Australasian Jam and BSC Footwear the taxpayer adopts the market selling value in the export market as the value of the stock on hand at year, that is, $220. The position will be, in the first year of income a deduction is claimed for the cost of the trading stock purchased, that is, $100; and $220 is added to income as being the 'inflated' market selling value of that stock on hand at the end of the first year. Thus, an amount of $120 is included in the taxpayers' taxable income in the first year of income as the trading stock adjustment in year one.
  6. Assume now the stock is sold in the second year in the export market for $220, and there is no adjustment in accordance with Australasian Jam to adjust market selling value to the market in which the taxpayer ordinarily operates.
  7. In the second year, being the year of sale, the taxpayer brings the sales proceeds of $220 to account as income and is allowed a deduction of $220, because the item is included in stock on hand at the beginning of the year at a value of $220, which amount is subtracted from stock at the end of the year because it is no longer on hand. Thus, the second year the deduction, by way of $220 included in opening stock, matches the income from the sale of $220 and no net amount is included in income in the second year. But the profit on sale of $120 is returned in full in year one. In this situation the taxpayer anticipates the profit for tax purposes in year one.
  8. Suppose instead the item is sold in year two in the domestic market for $160 and there is still no adjustment in accordance with Australasian Jam to use the domestic market being the market in which the taxpayer ordinarily sells its goods.
  9. Thus, in year two the taxpayer will obtain a deduction for $220 through the item of stock being included at $220 in stock on hand at the beginning of the year. The sale proceeds of $160 will be returned as income and there will be a net loss on sale of $60 in the second year. The correct net profit is returned over two years, namely, income of $120 in year one and a loss of $60 in year two.
  10. Suppose now the Commissioner discovers the incorrect market valuation and, in accordance with the Applicants' submissions closing stock for the first year should remain unadjusted but the Commissioner should amend the opening stock figure for the second year.
  11. On that scenario the taxpayer will, as in the example above, return $120 in the first year. In the second year when the sale occurs in the export market at $220, the sale proceeds of $220 will be returned as income but the taxpayer will only be allowed a deduction of $160 being the now amended opening stock figure.
  12. Thus in year two the taxpayer will pay tax on that article of $60 and will pay tax in year one on $120. Double taxation results because the taxpayer is not allowed in the second year a deduction for the difference between the closing stock figure of the article (that is $220) and the opening stock figure in year two being $160.
  13. The same problem occurs if the item is sold in the domestic market at $160 in the second year. Sale proceeds of $160 are returned as income and a deduction is allowed in respect of the adjusted opening stock figure of $160. The article sells at a profit of $60 but, $120 has been included assessable income in year one as being the inflated unadjusted market value figure of stock on hand of $220.
  14. Thus, where the proper value of stock on hand is less than the taxpayer's market selling value, then by only adjusting the opening stock figure and leaving the closing stock figure unamended, double taxation results to the taxpayer.
  15. In the converse situation where closing stock is under valued then by making an adjustment to increase opening stock to a proper market selling value the amount of the uplift escapes tax completely.
  16. This arises in the example above if it is assumed that the domestic market selling value is $220 and the export market selling value is $160. If the taxpayer incorrectly uses the export market value of $160 as the market selling value, then closing stock on hand at the end of year one is included back in income (less the opening stock figure for that year) at $160. But if the error is discovered and the Applicants' contention is accepted, the opening stock figure in year two is $220, and the taxpayer obtains a deduction for that amount by way of the $220 figure being subtracted from closing stock at the end of year two. Effectively a deduction is taken for $220 in year two, but only $160 is included in year one as income.
  17. If these matters were raised in objection or at the hearing then expert evidence could have been adduced through Mr. McClintock as to the proper accounting treatment of adjusting opening stock but not closing stock at the end of the previous year.

Construction of the 1936 Act and windfall gains and unexpected losses

  1. It is apparent from the above illustrations that the acceptance of the Applicants' contention can lead to either a windfall gain or a loss to the taxpayer. Such an intention is contrary to the legislative intention as expressed in the extrinsic materials.
  2. Authority exists that a Court should be disinclined to adopt a construction that has as its consequence unanticipated "windfall gains and unexpected losses": see Commercial Union Australia Mortgage Insurance Company Limited v FC of T 96 ATC 4854.
  3. In that case the taxpayer carried on a mortgage insurance business; whereby it would indemnify mortgagees against the risk of loss, in return for premiums. Although the indemnity extended over the term of the mortgage, the taxpayer company received the whole of its premium before or in the first year of the mortgage. For accounting and income tax purposes the taxpayer would apportion the premium progressively over the term of the mortgage.
  4. On subsequent advice, the taxpayer sought to change the method of apportionment, resulting in more of the premium being earned in the first 4 years, with less in later years. The change in method would create a windfall to the taxpayer, as some premium income of earlier years would escape taxation, because the Commissioner would be precluded from increasing assessable income in those years (1992 and earlier years), though, on the taxpayer's analysis the Commissioner was obliged to reduce the assessable income of the later years (1993 and following).
  5. In response to this, Lindgren J stated at 4861 –

"Notwithstanding CUAMIC's concession that the whole of an amount of premium received is ultimately to be returned as assessable income and its contention that the only difference between it and the Commissioner is one of timing, the position contended for by it can lead to a different result. It can lead to the escape from assessment of premium income which has been received, a result not intended by the legislation."

  1. The same outcome follows from acceptance of the Applicants' contention as to the trading stock adjustment. At page 4862 his Honour referred to the statutory scheme of the lodgement of returns, the making of an assessment being the ascertainment of taxable income (sec 166), the service of a notice of assessment (sec 174), and the validity of the assessment is not affected by any procedural irregularity (sec 175). His Honour then set out the terms of sec 177(1) and continued:

There has, of course, been no relevant proceeding on a review or appeal relating to the assessments of years preceding the year ended 30 June 1993 in the present case.

In my view, the scheme of the provisions to which I have referred is that in the absence of a proceeding under Part IVC of the TAA on a review or appeal relating to the assessments for earlier years, the amount assessed as premium income earned in those years is correct, leaving the balance to be earned in the year ended 30 June 1993 and future years. An alternative way of expressing this conclusion is to say that the scheme of those provisions is that CUAMIC can succeed only if its present appeal in relation to the year ended 30 June 1993 is associated with a proceeding on a review or appeal which would have the effect of increasing commensurately its assessable income, and therefore its taxable income, in respect of the earlier years."

  1. Finally, Lindgren J concluded at 4865 –

"In any event, in accordance with the scheme of the relevant provisions of the 1936 Act which I attempted to explain earlier, CUAMIC's assessable and therefore taxable income for the year ended 30 June 1993 must be ascertained in a manner not inconsistent with the proposition that the assessments have been duly made in the earlier years."

  1. In the absence of any appeal or review being made by the Applicants for the year ended 30 June 1995, (i.e. the year immediately prior to the year in which the Applicants are seeking to increase its opening balance for trading stock), the production of a certified copy of the Applicants' assessments for the year ended 30 June 1995 under subsection 177(1) of the 1936 Act is conclusive evidence that the notices of assessment for the year ended 30 June 1995 that the amount and all the particulars of the assessment are correct.
  2. In the Commissioner's submission, the decision in Kirkpatrick v CIR (1962) 13 NZLR 49 is indistinguishable and directly on point. It is the decision relied upon in Gunn's commentary for the proposition that the opening value "must correspond" with the value on hand at the end of the preceding year.
  3. In that case the taxpayer, a sheepfarmer, adopted the simple expedient of representing to the Revenue that he had no unsold wool on hand at the end of each income year. Income was understated because part of his profit was represented in stock on hand. He had adopted the practice for ten years or more.
  4. The subterfuge was discovered on his death in 1958 and the Revenue issued assessments back to the 1948 income year commencing on 1 April 1947. It seems the relevant New Zealand Act permitted amendment for ten years.
  5. The question raised was, what was the value of his stock on hand (unsold wool) at the beginning of the 1948 year of income. The taxpayer's return for the 1947 year showed the value of closing stock on 31/3/1947 as "Nil". The taxpayer's 1948 return showed the opening value of stock as "Nil". However, the actual wool on hand at 31 March 1947/1 April 1947 was known, as a fact, to be £1842. The value of wool on hand at the end of the 1948 year was £2,505. The Revenue's assessing action for 1948 was to include the value of closing stock at £2,505 as income and subtract "Nil" as the opening stock as per the 1947 and 1948 returns (closing and opening values respectively).
  6. Barrowclough CJ summarized the assessing action at page 50.7 as:

"the fact that the return for that year showed no wool in hand must therefore be conclusive evidence that on 31st March, 1947, the taxpayer had no wool in hand: that in other words, for the purposes of the Act, the taxpayer must be deemed to have no wool in hand on 31st March, 1947, even though we know that he had wool in hand worth £1842".

  1. So too, in the Applicants' position. If Mr. Mitchell's write downs are inappropriate, the Applicants will have on hand as closing stock on 30 June 1995 and opening stock on hand at 1 July 1995 a higher value of Aged Stock.
  2. Barrowclough CJ then set out sec 26 of the Act which corresponds to sec 177(1) of the 1936 Act. He relied upon the decision of Sir John Salmond in Anson v IRC (1922) NZLR 330 at 338 for the proposition "the assessments of the appellant in previous years, therefore, not having been objected to in the statutory time and manner, are conclusive against him."
  3. At page 51.5 Barrowclough CJ reached the conclusion that the value of closing stock on 31 March 1947 was a particular of the taxpayer's return and was also a particular of the assessment which issued in conformity with that return. It followed by the equivalent of sec 177(1) that the taxpayer was "estopped from disputing" the assessment, for the 1947 year ending 31 March 1947, and all the particulars thereof (including the value of stock on hand valued at "Nil").
  4. The learned Chief Justice then referred to sec 98(3) of the New Zealand Act (at page 52.5) which provision is relevantly indistinguishable from the terms of sec 29. It refers to "the value of trading stock ... to be taken into account at the beginning of any income year shall be its value as at the end of the last preceding income year."
  5. Barrowclough CJ then concluded (at page 52.5) as follows:

"the Commissioner was bound by the statute to act on the basis that the value of the wool in hand at the beginning of that year was its value at the end of the preceding year i.e. as at 31st March, 1947. Its value then is conclusively deemed to be nil and I cannot see how the Commissioner could lawfully avoid taking it into account as being of no value at all on 1st April, 1947. This is precisely what he did and I am of the opinion that he was right. ...

[The Commissioner] was required by the statute to take the wool into account at the beginning of the year at the same value as it had at the end of the preceding year and both he and the taxpayer are estopped from saying that its value at the end of the preceding year was other than nil" (Emphasis added)

  1. The decision in Kirkpatrick, and decision of the Sir John Salmond for the Court of Appeal in Anson, are correct and indistinguishable. The approach in those decisions is consistent, and in fact on all fours with, the decision of Lindgren J in Commercial Union Australia Mortgage (supra). It is true that Lindgren J does not use the expression "estopped". But his Honour specifically refers to sec 177(1) and says the later year assessment must be "not inconsistent" with the proposition the earlier assessments have been duly made.
  2. There is nothing in the decision of Hill J in Oates v FC of T 91 ATC 4060 that is inconsistent with the above analysis. That decision concerned carry forward losses. There was not the requirement for consistency as between two income years intimately linked and entwined through the statutory imperative of the value of closing stock for one year and the opening stock for the next being the same, as there was in Kirkpatrick, Anson and by analogy in Commercial Union.
  3. The question in Oates was whether the carry forward loss was a deduction in the year in which there was income to offset against the loss. Of necessity, that question of deductibility has to be adjudged in the income year when the loss is brought to account. The Commissioner's assessing action in the loss year cannot be decisive of the deductibility in the income year.
  4. One further matter requires comment. The Commissioner's assessing action in Kirkpatrick, Anson and in the instant case here, does not involve double taxation. In Kirkpatrick, an adjustment of £2,505 was made and there was no recognition of the factual value of stock on hand (£1,842) at the commencement of the year (it was disclosed as "Nil").
  5. But in an ideal world, that amount should have been brought to account as income as the closing stock in the preceding year. Unlike the Applicants' contention, there is, on the Commissioner's approach, no "windfall gains and unexpected losses". The line is drawn properly with opening stock at the beginning of the first year of adjustment fixed, as per the closing stock from the prior assessment. That is, after all, what Barrowclough CJ meant by both the Commissioner and the taxpayer being estopped from disputing the opening amount and what Lindgren J meant by the later assessment being "not inconsistent" with the earlier assessments duly made.
  6. There is no double tax or slippage on the Commissioner's analysis. At a point in time, the value of the opening stock at the beginning of the first year of adjustment is fixed, and thereafter the valuation of stock on hand at the end and commencement of each succeeding year proceeds on a proper basis.
  7. The practical effect of the Commissioner's adjustment is that the omitted income from improper closing stock valuations made in earlier years is brought to account in the year of the first adjustment. That is precisely the result in Anson: there the increase in value of livestock over time (brought about because the taxpayer adopted in each year a fixed low arbitrary value) was assessed in one amount, in the year of disposal, rather than incrementally in each year whilst the stock was on hand. But, on the Commissioner's approach, as in Kirkpatrick, Anson and the instant case, the proper amount of income is brought to account and most importantly, there are no "windfall gains and unexpected losses".
  8. Accordingly, the amount of closing stock on hand in the prior year is the value ascertained under the Act, and is the value to be used for the opening stock in the following year.
  9. Finally, if the Applicants' contention is accepted, it is a literal interpretation that has unlikely or arbitrary consequences. In AGC (Advances) Ltd v F C of T 75 ATC page 4057, Mason J observed at 4072 –

"If this conclusion involves a departure from the literal meaning of the words used it is to be justified by reference to the context and to the unlikely and arbitrary consequence which would flow from the adoption of a literal interpretation. That a loss having its origin in the course of carrying on a business for profit should only be deductible whilst the business is still in operation, though the loss may not be ascertained until a later date, seems to be a strange result; so strange indeed that an intention to bring it about should not be imputed to the Legislature when the statute is susceptible to a sensible interpretation."

The decision in Australasian Jam

  1. The Applicants' contention is, in addition to being, contrary to the legislative intent, productive of windfall gains and unexpected losses, and inconsistent with a proper construction of the Act, also directly contrary to the observations of Fullagar J in Australasian Jam.
  2. In Australasian Jam Co Pty Ltd v FC of T [1953] HCA 52; (1953) 88 CLR 23 at page 26 to 27 Fullagar J said as follows:

'It is on s. 31 that the present cases primarily turn. The section in terms allows to the taxpayer considerable freedom of choice. He may adopt one method of valuation for one part of his stock, and another method for another part. And he is not bound to adhere from year to year to any method of valuation for any part of his stock: he may change the basis as to the whole or any part of his stock from year to year at will. On the other hand, the section is imperative in that it requires him to adopt for each article of his stock one or other of the three prescribed bases of valuation. He is not at liberty to adopt some other basis of his own. And s. 29 requires that the value at which his stock is brought into account at the beginning of a year shall be the value at which it was brought into account at the close of the preceding year: In other words, the opening figure of any year must be identical with the closing figure of the preceding year.' (Emphasis added)

  1. The decision of Mr. Cotes in 14 CTBR, Case 10, predates the decisions in Australasian Jam, Kirkpatrick and Commercial Union Australia Mortgage. The decision is directly inconsistent with the decisions in Australasian Jam, and Kirkpatrick, both of which are decisions of superior Courts. Mr. Cotes did not have the benefit of being taken to the legislative history of sec 29 of the 1936 Act, or have access to the extrinsic materials, or the commentary by Gunn or the decision of Vaisey J in Steel Barrel Co Ltd v Osborne. The decision in Case No. 10 is contrary to the weight of authority and should not now be followed. The Applicants submission in respect to the notional adjustment of the opening stock in the 1996 year is without proper foundation.
  2. Similarly, the Federal Tax Reporter at paragraph [17-555] follows Case No. 10 and regards the observations of Fullagar J as per incuriam on that point. However as above, the CCH observations, in turn, are made without reference to the decisions in Kirkpatrick, Anson, Steel Barrel v Osborne, the commentary in Gunn as well as, the legislative history of sec 29 and the extrinsic materials.
  3. It is instructive to note that the Applicants' written submissions, in a section other than dealing with the observations of Mr. Cotes, accepts expressly the Commissioner's contentions concerning sec 29. The Applicants have submitted in paragraph 12 as follows:

"Section 31(1) in terms allows the taxpayer considerable freedom of choice. In regard to the 3 available options, the taxpayer is not limited to adopting one basis only for all articles but may choose a different basis of value for different articles of trading stock. And subject to the overriding requirement of s.29 that the value at the beginning of the year must be the same as that adopted at the end of the previous year, the taxpayer is not bound to maintain the adopted basis or mix of bases in subsequent years." (Emphasis added)

  1. Paradoxically, the authority cited in support of paragraph 12 by the Applicants is Australasian Jam Co Pty Ltd v FC of T [1953] HCA 52; (1953) 88 CLR 23 at 26-27 per Fullagar J. which is the very passage set out above and relied upon by the Commissioner.

Mr. Mitchell's evidence about market value

  1. The Applicants' case on the valuation of aged stock is solely dependent upon valuations undertaken by Mr. Mitchell. The Applicants say (paragraph 34 written submissions) that "the procedure developed by Mr. Mitchell for establishing a write down percentage for Aged Stock is not shown to be inappropriate or unreasonable by any evidence led for the Commissioner".
  2. Although this statement inverts the evidential onus the submission implicitly recognizes the justification for the write down percentage procedure rests solely with Mr. Mitchell's alleged expertise. It is stated (written submissions paragraph 37) that "Mr. Mitchell's write down percentage procedure was developed to address the problem of aged stock; there is no other currently accepted or alternative and practical procedures suggested by any valuer experienced in determining closing stock value of trading stock in the mass produced jewellery wholesale/retail market" (Emphasis in original)
  3. The Commissioner's primary submission on Mr. Mitchell's evidence is that the Tribunal cannot be satisfied the discount percentages arrived at in Mr. Mitchell's stock assessment in 1993 and 1995 are the product of a bona fide valuation process.
  4. Further, the discount percentages intended to be applied to stock on reaching 18 months of age, to be further discounted again at 36 months of age, did not result from a process which was calculated to result in the ascertainment of the market selling value of stock on hand, and could not be utilised by the Applicants as a basis for valuing their trading stock at year end, as at 30 June 1996 or 1997.
  5. It would also be open to the Tribunal to conclude that Mr. Mitchell never intended to engage in any bona fide valuation process at all, and that he arrived at discount percentages for so-called Aged Stock on a completely arbitrary basis, which had no relevance or relationship to reality, but was constructed with the deliberate intention of constructing purported market selling value for stock when he knew that the figures arrived at were not the end product of a bona fide valuation process.
  6. If the Tribunal agrees with any of the alternative ways of viewing Mr. Mitchell's evidence, as submitted by the Commissioner, it follows necessarily that the Applicants have not adopted one of the three alternatives set forth in section 31 of the 1936 Act for the valuation of trading stock in the years under review and the Commissioner's decisions on the objections must be upheld.
  7. Jade was an entity interposed between genuine wholesalers and Allay Pty Ltd as a retail entity. It was associated with or "captive" to Allay. Allay Pty Ltd was to all intents and purposes the only customer of Jade.
  8. However Mr. Mitchell had proceeded with his valuations on the assumption of dealing with a wholesaler which was in no way connected with the retailer (TC page 12.25 - 12.37). In fact he agreed that his valuation would not really apply if the retailer was to all intents and purposes the only client of the wholesaler (TC page 13.11-TC page 13.21). Moreover, his valuations were formulated on the basis of an assumed arm's length arrangement between a wholesaler and a retailer and not a sale on consignment (TC page 14.30-15.1).
  9. If as the Commissioner submits the arrangements between the Applicants, as the Jade partnership, and Allay Pty Ltd as retailer, cannot be regarded as an arm's length arrangement, then the very foundation for Mitchell's alleged valuations is destroyed.
  10. Mr. Mitchell has admitted the basis of his valuation is destroyed and he would not have made his valuations for Jade and Allay in that manner had he known of the non arms length arrangement. He was asked whether he would have issued his valuations in the same way, had he known that the wholesaler and the retailer were really one and the same person in economic terms. He admitted in answer to that question "If they were one entity, sir, no." (TC page 21.4).
  11. His specific evidence on this point was that he was instructed to undertake the valuation upon the assumption that there was an arm's length transaction between wholesaler and retailer, where the wholesaler was acting entirely independently of the retailer and where the entities were not controlled by the same people (TC page 14.36-15.1). He further made the assumption that the price of the goods sold was negotiated as a result of hard bargaining between willing but not anxious vendor and willing but not anxious purchaser (TC page 7.15-7.30).
  12. In fact Mr. Mitchell agreed that if he had to consider a valuation of stock in circumstances where there was an association between the wholesaler and the retailer he would treat the entity as one entity as opposed to two (TC page 9.3-9.10). To him this was a valuation situation which was in effect a "whole different ball game" and "that would be a completely different ball game" (TC page 9.10 and 9.15). This was because the need for the wholesaler to get rid of stock simply disappeared and that affected the situation because the goods would then be in a retail environment where they can be heavily discounted by up 70-80% of the normal retail price without incurring a loss (TC page 11.5-11.20).
  13. The Commissioner next submits, Mr. Mitchell's valuations which yielded up the stock percentage write downs were premised upon a hypothetical situation which never occurred in the reality of Jade's trading activities with Allay. Shortly put, his valuations of so-called "Aged Stock" assumed that at the 18 month and 36 month cut of points, aged stock was no longer saleable by Jade as wholesaler to Allay as retailer, but instead that the stock would have to be sold back up the distribution chain to stone merchants or bullion dealers, that in those circumstances the profit margins would be lost in sales back to the trade, thus justifying the discount percentages. Ultimately at the 36 month point the stock would only be suitable for scrap value or melting down (see for example exhibit A17 letter from Mr. Mitchell to Gibson dated 24 February 1993 which represented the English language version of Exhibit TM08 to Mitchell's affidavit TB page 647.25).
  14. Mitchell explained his rationale with respect to the gold content as follows:

(i) that after 18 months stock had passed its useful life and was no longer saleable and ultimately it became completely unsaleable (TB page 648.10); and

(ii) selling the gold back to the bullion dealer to be refined and reprocessed, results in the loss of the benefit of the labour content as well as losing 50% of the value of the gold (TB page 648.13-648.19).

  1. Mitchell explained his rationale with respect to the precious stones component follows:

(i) in relation to stones, a jeweller selling those back to the stone merchant gives rise to a loss of 20% of the uplift or mark up charged by the stone merchant to the jeweller (TB page 648.20-648.27); and

(ii) as a second consideration, because the stone merchant could buy from his own sources further back down the distribution chain, that warranted a further discount of 30% to induce the stone merchant to repurchase the stone from the jeweller (TB page 648.28-648.33).

  1. At the end of 18 months if the stock is still on hand it had to be valued looking at the components of the stock namely the gold and the stones (TB page 649.5).
  2. Mr. Mitchell agreed that the integral elements justifying the discount in those circumstances was that the best the wholesaler would ever do was to sell the goods back to the refiner and the stone merchant (TB page 649.20). Again this was the methodology referred to in Mitchell's letter to Mr. Hobbs of the ATO, which was in a similar format to Exhibit A17 (see Mr. Mitchell's affidavit Annexure "TM10" p.64 and TB page 651.25-651.30).
  3. Mr. Mitchell agreed that the assumptions adopted in TM04 (see Mr. Mitchell's affidavit page 21) which was again an English version of TM08 and in the same terms as the letter of 18 September 1997, was that at the end of 18 months all that could be done was to break the item into its component parts, sell the stones back to the stone merchant and sell the gold back to a refiner (TB page 653-654; TB page 700-701, TB page 702.20-702.24, TB page 704.32-704.36; TB page 705.27; TB page 707.16-707.25).
  4. Yet the evidence clearly discloses that Jade never operated in that market; and never sold goods back to stone merchants or gold refiners. Indeed it was common ground that in the relevant years merely a handful of items at retail level sold for less than retail cost, that is, actual wholesale cost from an independent wholesaler (TA page 58.13-58.18, and TA59.8-59.12). There were no sales by Jade to any entity other than Allay except for an insignificant number of sales in the month of January 1997 (TB page 61, TB page 63.6).
  5. It is next submitted that the Tribunal will find in any event that the basis upon which Mr. Mitchell arrived at his discount percentages in valuing trading stock has not been demonstrated to have been based upon any professional and reliable foundations. Accordingly, his so-called expert opinion is worthless, unreliable and an unsafe basis for concluding that the Applicants' trading stock could properly be brought to account on the basis of his valuation. Specifically Mr. Mitchell's expert opinion is bereft of primary factual material and silent on the validity of assumptions made.
  6. The methodology which Mr. Mitchell explains in-chief (TB page 552.14-552.27) asserts that surveys were obtained in order to calculate the applicable discounts at both wholesale and retail levels.
  7. However, nowhere in his affidavit nor in any of the other evidence is there any result of market research, the nature of the research, the methodology adopted or the precise content of the research (TB page 579.14-580.20). Notwithstanding his agreement that it was a fundamental tenet of his procedure to be transparent in his approach to valuation, he produced no worksheets, no source materials, no information which might corroborate his alleged methodology (TB page 599.11-600.11).
  8. Setting aside the veracity of TM08 (to which separate submissions will be addressed) there were 23 categories of goods not covered by the methodology set forth in TM08 (TB page 621.1-622.2). Mr. Mitchell produced no source material in relation to any of these categories, nor was he able to give any exposition at all about the basis upon which he arrived at discount percentage factors for those 23 categories (TB page 621.18, 621.29, 621.35).
  9. Notwithstanding that Mitchell was asked to produce documents on subpoena he produced no information which enabled the Tribunal to conclude what the alleged surveys may have shown and he produced no working papers or other documentation in relation to surveys (TC page 15.7-15.19).
  10. When he was pressed to explain how the survey information was utilised in order to arrive at particular discount percentages, in particular by reference to TM08, he was ultimately driven to agree that there was no retail market survey information at all in TM08 (TC page 23.24-14.3).
  11. He made one of many concessions about errors in his affidavit as to whether the market research surveys were wholesale or retail (TC page 18-20, TC page 24.17-24.21).
  12. Finally he agreed that none of the so-called information from the industry was reproduced in his affidavit or produced in answer to a notice to produce (TC page 29-30.2).
  13. He then conceded that a search high and low in the affidavit for basis upon which valuations for categories other than gold and jewellery will not enable any information to be found on that topic and that was completely unsatisfactory (TC page 39.18-39.22). In short, the affidavit, as Mitchell agreed, simply failed to incorporate any information enabling the Tribunal to determine the process by which he arrived at any valuation (TC page 39.27).
  14. The Commissioner further submits that Mr. Mitchell did not undertake a genuine valuation of so-called Aged Stock nor could he do so simply by reference to stock sheets provided to him by Peter Gibson. Mr. Mitchell agreed that the stock lists provided did not contain sufficient information to enable him to undertake proper valuation of stock according to industry recognised guidelines (TC page 47.24- 47.30).
  15. Quite apart from methodology involved in valuing gold or silver or jewellery, Mr. Mitchell agreed that he did not bring any expertise to bear to work out discount percentages in relation to other categories (TB page 621.18-622.2, specifically, TB page 621.15, 621.17,621.32, 621.35 and 622.2).
  16. When he suggested in his affidavit and in evidence that it would be possible to value items of jewellery simply by reference to stock numbers and identification he became spectacularly unstuck discussing the topic of cultured pearls. He agreed that in terms of valuation it was necessary to know the size of the pearls, the lustre, and the match of the pearls, all of which would require visual examination (TB page 634.28-635.27).
  17. Mr. Mitchell, of course, twice asserted that in the case of pearls there was in fact no write down (TB page 634.31 and 635.25). He was then confronted with his own analysis writing down pearls 50% after 18 months and the same percentage again after 36 months (TB page 635.18-636.7). He allowed himself to be drawn to a point of ridicule when he agreed with the suggestion "do you say there was some rapid decline in the pearl market since 1995" to which he answered, "yes I'm afraid there has, yes, there has" (TB page 637.11-637.12).
  18. He was then referred immediately to paragraph 42 of his affidavit which stated that "since 1995 pricing levels in the wholesale jewellery industry have remained generally static" (TB page 637.22-TB page 638). Although he would not accept that this was a "howling" error he conceded "it is an omission, yes, and I do apologise" (TB page 639.12).
  19. The Commissioner further submits that Mitchell's evidence about his valuation methodology, his explanations for arriving at his discount percentages, was riddled with error such as to make it unreliable. Specifically there is sufficient in the cross examination to enable the Tribunal to conclude that there was a complete absence of genuine valuation in the process undertaken.
  20. TM08 is a prime example of the insuperable difficulties with Mr. Mitchell's evidence.
  21. In paragraph 29 of his affidavit it was addressed as if it was the contemporaneous record showing how the discount percentages for all categories of stock were arrived at.
  22. In oral evidence in-chief this was moderated to being "an example of the actual calculations that we do on the stock items, on the wholesale stock items" (TB page 557.12), and it was asserted to have been done in January 2000 (TB page 557.17).
  23. In cross examination Mitchell agreed that in plain terms paragraph 29 of his affidavit suggested that TM08 was the table used to calculate the write downs dated 22 February 1993 and 20 June 1995 (TB page 602, particularly 602.30).
  24. Confronted with the terms of paragraph 29, as suggesting a contemporaneous document used to calculate the markdowns Mr. Mitchell agreed that it was put "badly" in his affidavit and he also agreed that it had been put "atrociously" and it was "absolutely incorrect" (TB page 603.5, TB page 603.12, TB page 603.13, TB page 603.20).
  25. Mr. Mitchell was then challenged about TM08 having been completed in January 2000 by reference to Annexure D to his affidavit in the Pontifex proceedings in September 1998 (TB page 604-TB page 605.18). He resolutely asserted that the document had been prepared in January 2000 because he had gone through exactly the same exercise at that time and the figures worked out exactly the same (TB page 605.24).
  26. It was then pointed out to him that an error in TM08 was identical to the error appearing in Annexure D to the Pontifex affidavit. When it was suggested that it was in fact the same document, he denied again, saying "no, my updating was done in the early part of 2000" (TB page 630-631.14). It was then demonstrated to Mr. Mitchell that the figures he had picked up in TM08 were exactly the same figures as appeared at page 22 of his affidavit (TB page 663-664). It was suggested to him that he had picked up the same figures as appeared in the JATVC publication of June 1997 in some instances. He would only agree that they were the same figures (TB page 668.31, TB page 669.19-669.23, TB page 670.12-670.20). Finally, he conceded that to his best recollection was that TM08 was put together in 1997 (TC page 27.15); more specifically in June 1997 (TC 28.11-28.15).
  27. Mr. Mitchell then agreed that, had he prepared TM08 in January 2000 (as he had earlier asserted), the process would have involved him looking at the most up to date JATC figures and therefore looking at the November 1999 gold price figures (TC page 28.24-28.27). He then agreed that, returning to paragraph 29 of his affidavit, the last sentence should more accurately be expressed as suggesting that TM08 was a copy of a table prepared in June 1997 which he looked at again in 2000 (TC page 28.32).
  28. Thus TM08 was not a contemporaneous document, it was not prepared in January in 2000 and it was undeniably the same document as appears in Annexure D to Mr. Mitchell's affidavit in Pontifex. Moreover, it is a document which exemplifies a process of reasoning that after 18 months gold jewellery or jewellery containing the precious stones has to be broken down and sold back up to stone merchants or bullion dealers (TC page 29.17-TC page 29.22). It therefore appears highly likely that there was never any document prepared to support the alleged valuations undertaken in 1992 or 1995. Mr. Mitchell could not recall anything having been done by way of preparation by him of such a document in either of those years (TC page 66.11).
  29. The Commissioner submits that when Mr. Mitchell swore his affidavit paragraphs 27, 28 and 29 he did so to give the impression that TMO8 was a contemporaneous record of valuations undertaken in 1992 and 1995 when he knew that it was a document prepared by him in 1997.
  30. He maintained the pretence until giving evidence in-chief, when no doubt because he expected that he may be discovered, he then suggested that it was a document prepared in the year 2000. When it was ultimately shown to him and he agreed that it was a 1997 document his credibility was utterly destroyed.
  31. The Commissioner also points to the numerous examples of errors, inconsistencies, amendments and "apologies" offered up by Mr. Mitchell for various parts of his affidavit. Matters which are about to be illustrated leave the Tribunal in a position where it could have no confidence whatsoever in the reliability of Mitchell's evidence and it follows therefore that it can have no confidence in the reliability or veracity of Mitchell valuations.
  32. Firstly, Mr. Mitchell had great difficulty in working out whether he was undertaking a valuation on the basis of sales by independent wholesaler to independent wholesaler or retailer. Paragraph 59(c) of his affidavit originally contained reference to the discount percentage having been arrived as the price of goods sold by a wholesaler to another arm's length wholesaler. He said he conducted surveys with wholesalers (TC page 15.24), he was then referred to paragraph 28 of his affidavit which said that he conducted market surveys with retail jewellers (TC page 16.32). He was then forced to agree that there was nothing in the affidavit suggesting survey information had been obtained from wholesalers (TC page 17.12). He then said that he conducted surveys with both retailers and wholesalers. Then when asked to identify the information from surveys which informed TM08 he was unable to do so.
  33. Mr. Mitchell referred in paragraph 59(b)(ii) to the concept of "integral nexus". He then said that the first that he knew of integral nexus was when he read the affidavit (TC page 55.17). In fact when he was first confronted with the words "integral nexus" when they appeared in his affidavit in the statement "there's an integral nexus between retail selling price and the wholesale where goods are sold on consignment" he was asked "what do you say the nexus is" he answered (somewhat plaintively) "dear". After a stammering utterance he then (not for the first or last time) said "I would have to refer back to - I do apologise, I am a bit vague there" (TB page 611.10-TB page 611.17).
  34. It quickly emerged that he didn't have a clue what integral nexus meant (TB page 612-613.10) and it is as plain as a pike staff that this whole concept is something that infiltrated the affidavit. This is somewhat dramatically demonstrated when he was questioned about paragraph 59(b)(ii) and (iii) which forced him to admit that paragraph 59(b) was diametrically opposed to what he had been saying "a moment or two ago". When asked to face up to it, Mr. Mitchell said "it would appear to be" (TB page 616.24).
  35. In terms of Mr. Mitchell's expertise as a valuer, it was put to him that "integral nexus" it was not a concept that was "relevant or referred to in valuation" to which Mr. Mitchell replied "No, sir" (TC page 56.13).
  36. Finally, the Commissioner submits that underlying Mr. Mitchell's valuations is a false premise that, simply because particular stock items are on hand at the end of 18 months or the end of 36 months, does not, ipso facto demonstrate that the stock is unsaleable. That this premise is false is amply demonstrated by the analysis undertaken by Mr. Kosutic to which particular reference is made in paragraphs 324 and 325, below.
  37. Secondly, age of stock does not in fact affect its saleability. Again, this is shown in Mr. Kosutic's analysis at paragraph 401.
  38. Next, Mr. Mitchell's assessment rests solely on age and the premise early referred to does not enable it to be ascertained whether there might conceivably be another 20 or 30 identical items which did sell within the 18 months leaving only one item which celebrates its 18 month or 36 month birthday (TB page 585.21).
  39. Obviously enough in that scenario the fact that one item is on hand at the end of 18 months when 20 or 30 have sold in the previous 18 months tells you nothing at all about saleability.
  40. What is more compelling and cogent evidence and what tells the Tribunal more about saleability is the fact that Mr. Ciprian, an experienced retail jeweller and purchaser of stock, on occasions when the last item of Aged Stock was sold after reaching its 36th month birthday, immediately, or shortly thereafter, purchased for consignment identical replacement stock to that supposedly unsaleable item.
  41. Accordingly, the Commissioner submits that the Applicants have not discharged their onus of establishing that the assessments are excessive. Their case depends upon, as their written submissions at paragraphs 34 to 37 acknowledge, the intellectual integrity of the wholesale percentage write down mechanism developed by Mr. Mitchell. In the Commissioner's submission the process undertaken by Mr. Mitchell is irretrievably flawed and does not constitute a bona fide attempt to ascertain "market selling value" and for that reason alone the Applicants have not discharged their onus of proof.
  42. The evidence of each of Mr. Gerard and Mr. Sechos depends for its intellectual underpinnings on the integrity of the process undertaken by Mr. Mitchell. If Mr. Mitchell's process is irretrievably flawed then the evidence of Mr. Gerard and Mr. Sechos is similarly affected and not worth the proverbial hill of beans.
  43. The evidence of Mr. Alford and Ms. Foley shows that it is possible to value jewellery, but most assuredly not in the manner contemplated by Mr. Mitchell. Contrary to Mr. Mitchell, experienced valuers would not rely on the stock lists to provide sufficient information for accurate valuation, nor would they do such a valuation on a sight unseen basis, but rather a sampling technique would be adopted. Further, in Mr. Alford's opinion (paragraph 53 and following of his affidavit) it would be most unlikely that a retail jeweller would sell jewellery for scrap as assumed by Mr. Mitchell.
  44. There is no onus on the part of the Commissioner to positively establish what the Applicants' proper taxable income is, as a matter of fact, or indeed to adduce any evidence at all. In this case, however, in addition to the veritable plethora of internal inconsistencies and the irretrievably flawed nature of Mr. Mitchell's analysis, which is, without more, enough for the Commissioner to succeed, positive evidence has been adduced through Mr. Kosutic to further demonstrate the absurdity of Mr. Mitchell's analysis, including in particular, the so-called integral nexus.

Admissibility of Mr. Kosutic's evidence

  1. The Applicants at paragraph 59 of their submissions object to the evidence of Mr. Kosutic on the grounds of relevance and foundation. They assert, he is not an expert on the matters to which his statements are directed, and that he has focussed on retail sales by Allay subsequent to the balance date, and that Mr. Kosutic has not established the requisite qualifications and experience to design, assemble and interpret the statistical model that he has attempted.
  2. The Applicants assert (submissions paragraph 59) that 'Retail sales by Allay are not relevant to market selling value of Aged Stock in the hands of Jade as a wholesaler'. The Applicants assert that Mr. Mitchell's write-down percentages take into account sales experience prior to the relevant balance dates.
  3. Lest there be any doubt on the matter, and, notwithstanding that the concept of 'integral nexus' was a late insertion by the lawyers in Mr. Mitchell's affidavit, at paragraph 59(b)(ii) and (iii) this is what Mr. Mitchell had to say about retail selling price and the wholesale selling price of Aged Stock on consignment:

'There is an integral nexus between retail selling price and the wholesale selling price of aged stock especially where goods are sold on consignment. Wholesalers will take their cues from retailers in respect of the level of discounts to be applied to the wholesale of aged stock. For example, if an 18 month old diamond ring is being sold by the retailer at a 50% the wholesaler must discount the price of that item by at least 50% if he is to sell that same product to retailers.



The wholesale price must be written down by a proportional amount to what the retail selling price is written down by, otherwise no retailer will buy that stock from the wholesaler.' (Emphasis added)

  1. Again, so there can be no doubt about Mr. Mitchell's methodology, he has stated at paragraph 59(f)(i) and (ii) of his statement as follows:

'If after three years, where an item of stock would have been offered for sale during at least two Christmas periods (the peak retail period), an item has not sold, that item is simply not going to sell. In determining the value of such stock we assume the jeweller has taken active steps to sell the stock, such as applying significant discounts and the like. That being the case the only value that item has is scrap value.



If a retailer has an item of stock on its shelves that has not sold after three years, the likelihood is that retailer will not purchase that item from a wholesaler at, let alone for scrap value. A sale of stock which is three years old from a wholesaler to a retailer (or another wholesaler) for anything other than scrap value will be very unlikely.'

  1. Again, lest there be any doubt, Mrs. Ciprian at paragraphs 19 and 20 of her affidavit had this to say concerning the discounting of Aged Stock at the retail level:

'If an item of stock has been offered for sale during a Christmas trading period and has not sold it is highly unlikely that that item of stock will be saleable at its current price. Therefore once an item reaches 18 months in age that item will have been through one Christmas trading period and if unsold will need to be discounted to effect a sale. . . .



An item that has reached over 24 months old has been through two Christmas trading periods, and usually has been already marked down. If such an item has not sold, the chances of selling the item at its current, albeit already discounted, price is highly unlikely. Such items will need to be even further discounted if they are to sell. An item which is over 36 months has been through three Christmas trading periods, and if unsold is unlikely to ever sell. The best we can hope to recover from such an item is its scrap value. The longer the item remains on the shelf, the greater the loss to the business.'

  1. More specifically at paragraph 42 Mrs. Ciprian has stated as follows:

"From time to time 'sales' would be conducted for the purpose of encouraging the sale of aged stock.... Often the item would be marked down to an extent greater than the discounted amount which appeared on the stock sheet; ie the retail 'sale' price would be less than that calculated by the program." (Emphasis added)

  1. Notwithstanding the palpable deficiencies in Mr. Mitchell's analysis exposed above, the analysis conducted by Mr. Kosutic was a direct response to Mr. Mitchell's core assumption, that the retail value of Aged Stock diminished rapidly with age and, as a consequence, the wholesale value of that Aged Stock needed to be reduced on a proportionate basis.
  2. Using this proportional reduction approach, Mr. Mitchell determined that, across all categories of Aged Stock, the retail price achieved for that Aged Stock would diminish by approximately 70% and, as a consequence, the wholesale value of that Aged Stock on hand to the Applicants was also be reduced across all categories of Aged Stock by that very same percentage. The written down value of Aged Stock was its scrap or melted down value. Mrs. Ciprian claimed in evidence that 'I think we would be very lucky to get that amount' (TB page 53.4), that is the written down amount.
  3. What Mr. Kosutic has done by his analysis is to test that core assumption that retail value of Aged Stock diminishes rapidly with age and, as a consequence, the wholesale value needed to be reduced on a proportional basis. Mr. Kosutic's analysis shows that that core assumption is demonstrably false.
  4. The analysis undertaken by Mr. Kosutic is voluminous, though not complex. After all, all that he has done is to sort through the Applicants' own documents, that is, primarily their own stock reports. It is an analysis that would be open to the Tribunal to undertake, if so minded, in any event. This is because it is, at the end of the day, simply an inspection, identification and sorting of primary evidentiary material that is properly before the Tribunal.
  5. Notwithstanding that Mr. Kosutic's affidavit and annexures has been available since 10 January 2001 the Applicants have chosen not to produce any competing analysis of that primary evidentiary material and, thereby challenge the integrity of Mr. Kosutic's analysis.
  6. Mr. Kosutic's analysis is relevant and admissible and, more importantly, cogent and compelling.
  7. The primary source material examined by Mr. Kosutic was, of course, the business records and stock sheets of Allay and not the Applicants trading as Jade Wholesalers. Such sale reports by the Applicants trading as Jade Wholesalers were not made available to Mr. Kosutic (TC page 328.7) and such sales reports were called for (TC page 328.14). No such sales reports were produced to the Tribunal in response to the call.
  8. Mr. Kosutic's analysis is directed at the following matters:

(a) The Applicants went straight to the top rate write-down, discussed infra at paragraph 296 and following.

(b) In respect of Aged Stock the Applicants treated the economic "retail cost" as the aggregate of the original wholesale cost paid by the Applicants together with the sales tax paid on the written down value of the item, discussed infra at paragraph 305 and following.

(c) Aged Stock, in the 1996 and 1997 income years, on average was discounted at retail level by 14% and 18%, yet contrary to Mr. Mitchell's core assumption, the "market selling" value of Aged Stock was written down on average by 74% and 72% respectively, discussed infra at paragraph 315 and following.

(d) Mr. Mitchell undertook the valuation exercise for at least 10 jewellers and, in respect of each jeweller, arrived at almost identical write-down percentages in respect of each category, discussed infra at paragraph 339 and following.

(e) Items of Aged Stock were sold post 30 June 1996 in the 1997 income year, but were not shown on closing stock lists as at 30 June 1996, discussed infra at paragraph 354 and following.

(f) Items of Aged Stock, on occasions sold for a retail discount equal to or greater than Mr. Mitchell's write-down percentage, but the average age of such items was 7.43 years and not 12 months, 18 months or 3 years, discussed infra at paragraph 356 and following.

(g) Of nearly 4200 items of Aged Stock sold, only 18 were sold at a loss, that is, for an amount less than retail cost, discussed infra at paragraph 366 and following.

(h) In respect of all 1922 items of Aged Stock sold in the 1997 year, those items were, on average, written down by Mr. Mitchell by 66% of their cost, but such items were sold at retail for an average mark up of 112%, that is, 2.12 times their retail cost, discussed infra at paragraph 375 and following.

(i) Aged Stock was written down to the maximum amount (because the Applicants went straight to the maximum write-down) solely on the basis of its age, that is, on its 18th month anniversary irrespective of its sales pattern in that 18 month period. The "clock was not reset" by a sale, only a repurchase of the same stock, discussed infra at paragraph 380 and following.

(j) When Aged Stock was sold out, identical replacement stock was purchased, rather than the Applicants moving out of that difficult to move line of merchandize, discussed infra at paragraph 386 and following.

(k) Items of Aged Stock were sold at a discount at retail during sale months and subsequently sold, in later non sale months, for full ticket price, notwithstanding their further aging, discussed infra at paragraph 398 and following.
