(l) After 18 months the profile of Aged Stock held in the Katanning store mirrored the Albany and Ciprian stores, notwithstanding that, as a new store, the Applicants had an opportunity to stock it exclusively with fast moving stock, discussed infra at paragraph 394 and following.

  1. Each of these propositions are discussed in turn.

The top write-down rate

  1. By exhibit TMO3 (Mitchell affidavit page 19) stock in category one, being diamond rings greater than $500, was recommended to be written down by 54% after 18 months and 61% after 36 months. Exhibit BK88 (folio 2261 and following) contains the stock revaluation reports for Allay.
  2. However the Applicants have not implemented Mr. Mitchell's stock assessment. Rather the Applicants have gone straight to the top rate applicable to items aged 36 months and over and applied that rate immediately to stock aged 18 months (TC page 308.1 – 308.5).
  3. Thus for example, at folio 2261, the write-down percentages in category one for diamond rings over $500 was shown as 54% at 18 months and 61% at 36 months. However the Applicants have gone straight to a write-down percentage of 61% at 18 months and there is the handwritten annotation "54, 54, stet" setting out the correct percentages.
  4. Similarly by way of example in category 27 (bracelets and bangles) the write-down percentages were 50% and 75%. However the Applicants have gone straight to the 75% write down at 18 months and there is the handwritten correction "50" immediately adjacent to the incorrect write down of 75% at 18 months (folio 2261).
  5. The effect of the incorrect implementation of the write-down percentages is, of course, that the Applicants secure for themselves the maximum available write down at the earliest opportunity in contravention of Mr. Mitchell's own two step write down procedure. In the case of watches there is, of course, a third step involving an initial write down at 12 months.
  6. Whilst Mr. Kosutic was not aware of this accelerated write down procedure at the time of the making of the amended assessments it is a fact indicating at least carelessness on the part of the Applicants and is a relevant consideration that the Tribunal can take into account as part of its review of the additional tax by way of penalties.
  7. The assertion at paragraph 29 of the Applicants' written submissions that due to a misunderstanding by Mrs. Ciprian stock was not written down to the full extent recommended by Mr. Mitchell is wrong.

The calculation of economic or retail cost

  1. The records of the company Allay pick up in their actual records the concept of retail cost. This is also referred to as the economic cost (TC page 311.10). The retail cost, being the economic cost of a particular item, is the wholesale cost on acquisition by Jade plus the sales tax on the written down value (TC page 310.12).
  2. Thus, for example, BK93 (folio 3188 and following) contains a full list of the Aged Stock which was sold in the 1997 financial year and therefore on hand as at 30 June 1996. Thus, item one on folio 3188 shows stock number 030288 being purchased on 24 October 1993 at a wholesale cost of $273.60. It is on hand as at 30 June 1996 and, accordingly, has been written down by Mr. Mitchell's recommended percentage write down of 61%. The written down value is thus $106.70 being 39% (that is 100% written down by 61%) of $273.60. Sales tax is then calculated at the rate of 32% on that written down value, that is, 32% of $106.70 which equals $34.14.
  3. In paragraphs 29 and 31 of the Applicants' written submissions it is suggested that the sale by Jade to Allay was at "written down value plus the 6% uplift factor". That assertion in the Applicants' written submissions is wrong. In the example in the paragraph above, from folio 3188, sales tax (32% of $106.70 which equals $34.14) is calculated on the written down value only. There was no 6% cost uplift. Mrs. Ciprian is mistaken in her belief that "I have always thought that is how the program operated" (TB page 199.5)
  4. The retail cost or economic cost is the original wholesale cost of the item ($273.60) plus sales tax of $34.14 (that is 32% of $106.70) which two amounts add up to $307.74, which is the figure appearing in column eight line one at folio 3188 under the heading retail cost. The retail cost of the economic cost is thus the break-even point at which the aggregate economic unit consisting of Jade Wholesalers and Allay must sell the item to break-even. That is, they must cover the original acquisition cost together with the sales tax (TB page 219.8).
  5. For completeness, aged stock item number one on folio 3188 was sold on 16 July 1996 at a retail sale price of $695 which, though it represents a discount of 13% on the retail ticket price (folio 3188 column 10), nonetheless it represents a sale at a mark up of 126% over the retail cost or the economic cost.

Retail ticket price

  1. The full retail ticket price is in the nature of an ambit claim. The retail ticket price is marked up by a factor of 3 in the case of items worth between $0 and $500. In the case of higher priced items the mark up factor is reduced. Thus a jeweller at retail level is able to discount the ticket price without imperiling his profit (TC page 575.23 to 577.40).
  2. Wholesale profit margins are considerably smaller, somewhere in the region of 30% to 40 % (TC page 577.22). Thus, 27% off a retail mark up of 327% is vastly different to a 27% reduction in the wholesale price (TC page 577.19).
  3. As a practical matter, the other effect of adopting Mr. Mitchell's write-down percentages for Aged Stock is that sales tax is reduced in respect of the sale of Aged Stock items. If an Aged Stock item is sold at full retail ticket price, the Applicants make a super normal profit because sales tax is calculated on the written down value, but the retail ticket price is constructed on the assumption that sales tax will be 32% (or whatever is the applicable rate) on the wholesale cost (TC page 5769 to page 576.20).
  4. The jeweller would thus be able to reduce the ticket price of an Aged Stock item by the amount of the sales tax saving and still achieve his full undiminished gross profit margin.
  5. One surprising effect of adopting Mr. Mitchell's write-down percentages for Aged Stock is that the Applicants' business performs better by selling Aged Stock, at full ticket price, or alternatively offering some discount to the customer out of the sales tax saving on Aged Stock, than it does for selling new stock to customers at full ticket price.
  6. Mrs. Ciprian candidly admitted "we are actually generating more profit at retail level with aged stock, than with non-aged stock" (TB page 46.4). That profit is, of course, at the expense of the Revenue.
  7. Perversely, the Applicants' business performs better if it moth balls its stock for the perfunctory 12 or 18 month period. One is immediately reminded of the caustic observations of the High Court in the J Rowe and Sons case (supra) to the effect that the more the business grows the more its profit diminishes.

The integral nexus assumption

  1. Annexure BK98, results analysis 1, contains an analysis of the sale in the 1997 year of Aged Stock that was on hand as at 30 June 1996. The analysis appears at folio 3268 and following. A similar analysis appears in respect of the 1998 financial year at folio 3382 and following.
  2. The objective of results analysis 1 and 6 is to compare the percentage write down in Mr. Mitchell's stock assessment with the retail discount for the purposes of testing the integral nexus argument.
  3. Thus Mr. Kosutic 'wanted to see for example, if Mr. Mitchell wrote something by 50% whether the retail price was also reduced by 50%' (TC page 314.20).
  4. More particularly the analysis sought to test the proposition advanced by Mr. Mitchell and Mr. Gibson 'that the write downs were supportable because there was a relationship between percentage write downs at retail level being replicated at the wholesale level' (TC page 316.23).
  5. Thus, at folio 3268 the sales of Aged Stock in the 1997 financial year are set out in respect of the Ciprian store and the relevant sales for the Albany and Katanning stores appears at folios 3269.
  6. With respect to the Ciprian store, line 1 on folio 3268 shows that in July 1996 a total of 38 Aged Stock items were sold in the month (column 3). Of those 38 Aged Stock items, 27 were sold for full retail and 11 were sold for some retail discount (columns 4 and 5 respectively). In that month there were no Aged Stock items sold for a retail discount that was equal to, or greater than, Mr. Mitchell's wholesale stock write-down percentage (column 6). In percentage terms, for that month, 71% of sales of Aged Stock were at full retail price and 29% of sales were at some retail discount (columns 7 and 8 respectively).
  7. In respect of those 38 Aged Stock items sold in that month, Mr. Mitchell's average wholesale write-down percentage for those Aged Stock items sold was 70% (column 10). However, the average retail discount percentage for those Aged Stock items sold in that month was only 9%.
  8. The total figures for the Ciprian store for the 1997 financial year showed 850 Aged Stock items sold, of which 406 were sold for full retail price (some 48%) some 444 were sold for some retail discount (that is 52% of Aged Stock sales) and 41 items (5% of Aged Stock sales) were sold for a retail discount that was equal to or greater than the wholesale percentage write down.
  9. More particularly for the Ciprian store, for the whole of the 1997 financial year, Mr. Mitchell's average wholesale write down in respect of all Aged Stock items sold was some 74%. However the average retail discount given in respect of the sale of those Aged Stock items was only 18% (folio 3268).
  10. The aggregate figures for all three stores for the 1997 financial year appear at folio 3269. Thus across all stores, 1922 Aged Stock items were sold in the 1997 financial year, of which 962 were sold for full retail price, 960 items sold for some retail discount and there were 178 Aged Stock items that sold for a retail discount that was equal to or greater than the wholesale percentage write down (folio 3269 column 6). The discrepancy between the figures of 1922 and 1898 for Aged stock for 1997 is caused by the stock sheets missing at the time of the preparation of Mr. Kosutic's statement.
  11. Thus in percentage terms for the 1997 financial year, across all stores, the average wholesale write down percentage for the Aged Stock items sold was 72%. However, the average retail discount given in respect of those Aged Stock items sold was only 18% (folio 3269).
  12. The same analysis was conducted using actual dollar values rather than a percentage number of items.
  13. The analysis in actual dollar values for the 1997 financial year appears at folios 3270 and 3271. Thus, 1898 items of Aged Stock were sold, of which 961 were sold for full retail price and 937 were sold for some retail discount (columns 4 and 5 folio 3271). Those 1898 items of Aged Stock had a wholesale cost of $124,329.81 (folio 3271 column 6) and as at 30 June 1996 the written down value (that is, on the Applicants' analysis the "market selling value") of those 1898 Aged Stock items was $41,954.82 (folio 3271 column 7). The average wholesale write down percentage for those 1898 Aged Stock items was 66% (column 8, that is $41,954.82 is 34% of $124,329.81). The full retail ticket price of those 1898 Aged Stock items sold was $396,603.31 (column 9) and the aggregate retail selling price of those 1898 Aged Stock items was $290,646.29 (column 10). The average retail discount for those 1898 Aged Stock items was thus 27%.
  14. The corresponding analysis in respect of the 1998 financial year appears at folio 3382 and following. Thus, in terms of the actual number of Aged Stock items, some 2210 were sold in the 1998 year of which 1223 were sold for the full retail ticket price, some 987 were sold for some retail discount and 82 Aged Stock items were sold at a retail discount that was equal to or greater than the wholesale percentage write down (folio 3383 column 6).
  15. The average wholesale write-down percentage for all 2210 Aged Stock items sold in the 1998 year was a write down of 74%. However, the average retail discount percentage given in respect of all 2210 Aged Stock items sold was only 14% (folio 3383 column 11).
  16. The analysis of all Aged Stock sales in the 1998 financial year expressed using actual dollar values appears at folios 3384 and 3385. Thus, in summary form in respect of the three stores the 2210 Aged Stock items sold had an aggregate wholesale cost of $115,446.88 (column 6). The actual written down value of the 2210 Aged Stock items sold was $35,419.81 which represents an average wholesale write down percentage for all 2210 Aged Stock items sold of some 69% (folio 3385 column 8, that is $115,446.88 reduced by 69% equals $35,419.81). The full retail selling price (that is the ticket price) of the 2210 Aged Stock items sold was $382,701.24 (column 9) and the actual retail selling price achieved on the 2210 Aged Stock items sold in the 1998 financial year was $303,647.14 (folio 3385 column 10). Thus, the average retail discount in actual dollar values was only 21%.
  17. The results analysis at BK98 (folio 3268 and following) and BK112 (folio 3382 and following) demonstrate that the so-called integral nexus is, at best, without rational foundation, and at worst, total nonsense.
  18. Lest there be any doubt in that respect the relevant passages from paragraph 59 of Mr. Mitchell's affidavit are set out above together with paragraphs 19, 20 and 42 of Mrs. Ciprian's affidavit together with Mrs. Ciprian's oral evidence.
  19. By Mr. Mitchell's own admission the integral nexus argument was a late addition to his affidavit and, empirically, it is just plain wrong.
  20. The analysis undertaken by Mr. Kosutic was thorough and analysed the sale of approximately 4,200 Aged Stock items so as to ascertain if there was "some sort of pattern . . . which we could use as a guide to what they were worth' (TC 323.32).
  21. The analysis covered every single item of Aged Stock that was sold over the two year period (TC 324.25). And of course the analysis 'picks up every single item of Aged Stock that was sold no matter how long ago it was purchased' (TC 325.26).
  22. The sale of every Aged Stock item over that two year period is representative of what happens to Aged Stock (TC 324.35).
  23. Approximately 50% of Aged Stock items on hand at the beginning of a financial year will sell during the course of that year. Thus on 1 July 1996 there were approximately 4,500 Aged Stock items and approximately 2,000 were sold in the 1997 financial year (TC 327.3).
  24. It is instructive to compare the analysis above with the conclusion expressed in Pontifex Jewellers v FC of T 2000 ATC 4642 at page 4648 paragraph 16 that "on occasion, old stock, did sell for full original price, but this was not usual, ... [in] general I am satisified the retail sales could only have be effected at very substantial reductions." In the 1997 and 1998 financial years of the 4,132 items of Aged stock sold by the Applicants, the full retail ticket price was achieved on 2,185 occasions, that is 53% of the Aged Stock sales were for full price. Contrary to the finding in Pontifex, here it was the exact opposite, it was usual for the sale to be at full ticket price and it did not occur on occasion. Moreover the retail discount, either expressed as per number sold or dollar value, is a fraction of Mr. Mitchell's write-down percentage.

Comparison with ten other jewellery enterprises

  1. Mr. Kosutic, as part of his duties with the jewellery industry project had access to other stock assessments prepared by Mr. Mitchell for other jewellers. Ten were picked at random with the Applicants used as the template (Kosutic statement 10 January 2001 paragraph 37).
  2. Different jewellers allocated different category numbers to particular categories and goods. Thus, jeweller number 10, for example, might describe diamond rings over $500 as category 24 whereas the Applicants allocated category 1 for those items (TC 332.7).
  3. BK 75 (folio 376) sets out the comparative write-down percentages for each of the ten jewellers in respect of the particular category by its description, irrespective of the category number allocated by the particular jeweller.
  4. When the categories of each of the ten jewellers were synchronised it is then possible to compare Mr. Mitchell's write-down percentages for each of those ten jewellers.
  5. BK75 (folio 376) discloses that 'but where the categories were identical, the write downs were identical' (TC 332.17). Decimal points are used on occasions. But mere fractional differences cannot disguise the fact that there is uniformity in the write-down percentages. Thus, at folio 376 line one, in respect of the category of diamond rings over $500, the write-down percentages have a remarkable uniformity of 0, 54, and 61. One jeweller does, apparently, have the fractional percentage of 58.9.
  6. Paragraph 48 of Mr. Mitchell's affidavit of 26 April 2001 conveys the impression that the technical description of an item, for example, '9CTYGOYL8X6 GARNET 10X.01 DIA RIN' was relied upon by Mr. Mitchell and formed the basis of his write-down percentage.
  7. If, indeed the technical description for each item, as set out by way of illustration in paragraph 48 of his affidavit, was the basis of the percentage write down, then the write-down percentages for each of the ten sample jewellers should be different because each jeweller would be holding a different profile and quantity of stock (TC 334.25).
  8. However that is not the case. Virtually similar write-down percentages, if not outright identical ones, were arrived at by Mr. Mitchell by reference to the category or department.
  9. That Mr. Mitchell valued by category or department can be demonstrated by reference to sample jeweller number four. That jeweller adopted the apparently maverick practice of not describing his categories or departments in conformity with the stock standard categories or departments so readily understood by Mr. Mitchell (Mr. Kosutic's statement paragraph 43 and exhibits BK55 and BK56).
  10. The list of departments for sample jeweller four appears at BK56 (folio 342). The unique nature of the categories or department adopted by jeweller four is apparent when compared with the standard categories of the Applicants as set out in TMO2. Thus, for example, jeweller four has 79 categories as opposed to the Applicants' 40, and jeweller four has unusual categories such as category 52 (Pierre Cardin) and barometers/thermometers.
  11. It is instructive to observe Mr. Mitchell's reaction to the extraordinary categories nominated by jeweller four. Thus Mr. Mitchell to Mr. Gibson at exhibit BK55 (folio 341) as follows

'I have now completed an inspection of . . . jewellers stock cards and as we recently discussed it is very difficult for us to assess values. However I have listed below the various catalogues [sic] with the average percent discount that would apply to Aged Stock.

  1. Thereafter Mr. Mitchell provides write-down percentages for the stock standard 38 categories ordinarily encountered.
  2. In the second round valuation for jeweller four on 6 October 1994 Mr. Mitchell, when again confronted with the unusual categories and departments, wrote as follows

'I have recurved [sic] the stock report your reference 3594 but as the items are not grouped I have given discounts for the various product groups which your client apply'.

  1. Contrary to what Mr. Mitchell asserts, by inference, in paragraph 48 of his affidavit that the technical stock description was sufficient and each item was valued by reference to that stock description, Mr. Mitchell has not attempted to value each item individually with a percentage write-down for that jeweller for that item.
  2. He has simply provided jeweller four with the stock standard template and standard write-downs with the expectation that hopefully 'this will give you a basis for your assessment' and the template is something 'which your client can apply'.

Stock sold in the 1997 financial year but not listed as on hand at 30 June 1996

  1. An analysis of the sales records for Aged Stock sold in the 1997 year discloses 66 items of Aged Stock which are sold but which do not appear as being on hand as at 30 June 1996. The analysis is contained at BK96 (folio 3263 and following). No explanation is available and the analysis is included to show that for these items it was possible to calculate a wholesale cost or a written down value (folio 3263 columns 4 and 5). The retail cost of these 66 items is $3,572.62 (folio 3265) of which some $996.41 is accounted for in one diamond ring over $500 (line 38 folio 3264).
  2. The effect of the exclusion is de minimis.

Aged Stock selling for a retail discount equal to or greater than Mr. Mitchell's write-down percentage

  1. The basis of the valuation methodology and wholesale percentage write-downs arrived at by Mr. Mitchell is based exclusively upon the age of item in question. The relevant passages from Mr. Mitchell at paragraph 58(h)(ii) and paragraphs 19, 20 and 42 of Mrs. Ciprian's affidavit and her oral evidence is set out above.
  2. Of course, the milestone events selected by Mr. Mitchell occur at 12 months (in the case of watches) 18 months and 36 months.
  3. On 178 occasions Aged Stock items sold for a retail discount which was equal to or greater than the wholesale percentage write-down recommended by Mr. Mitchell (BK101 folio 3275 and following).
  4. Thus, for example, item stock number 230901 (line 1 on folio 3275) was purchased on 30 November 1991 for a wholesale cost of $46.97. It was subjected to a wholesale write down of 50% and accordingly its written down value was $23.49 (column 5). That item was sold on 4 October 1996, almost five years after the date of its acquisition, for a retail sale price of $60 (column 9) which represents a retail discount from the ticketed price of 73% (10). The retail cost (that is the economic cost) of the item was $54.49 so that, at a sale price of $60 the item was still sold at a profit.
  5. The point of the analysis in exhibit BK101 is to record those instances, where, if the integral nexus argument is to have any credence, the retail discount matches (and/or exceeds) the wholesale percentage write down (T338.15). What Mr. Kosutic was endeavouring to ascertain 'is how many times is the retail discount the same or greater than the wholesale write down because it's at the heart of their arguments' (TC 340.23).
  6. In respect of these 178 items of Aged Stock, which sold for a retail discount greater than the wholesale percentage write down advocated by Mr. Mitchell, nonetheless, the items sold at a profit. The retail cost of the 178 items was $17,457.83 and the sale price was $24,079.82 (folio 3281).
  7. Exhibit BK100 (folio 3274) contains an age profile in respect of these 178 items of Aged Stock where, in conformity with the integral nexus argument, the retail discount was equal to or greater than the wholesale percentage write down.
  8. The age profile discloses that six items only were two years old at the point of sale, 11 items were three years old on sale, and so on (folio 3274). Most significantly 29 items were aged six years or more on sale, 28 items were seven years or older, 30 items were eight years old on sale and another 30 items were nine years old on the day of sale. Aged Stock items between 6 to 9 years inclusive, in fact accounted for 117 items out of 178. Additionally, nine items were 16 or more years old on the day of sale.
  9. The average age of all 178 items of Aged Stock sold at a retail discount equal or greater than Mr. Mitchell's recommended wholesale write down was 7.43 years and not, most significantly, any of 12 months, 18 months or 36 months. However, it must be noted that there were many items of this age or older which sold for the full retail price. This analysis only covered items where the retail discount matched or exceeded the wholesale write down.
  10. The selection of milestone events at 12 months, 18 months and 36 months is arbitrary, accelerated, and premature.

Aged Stock sold for less than retail cost

  1. As set out above Mrs. Ciprian has stated in paragraphs 19, 20 and 42 of her affidavit that an item over 36 months has been through three Christmas trading periods "and is unlikely to ever sell [and] the best we can hope to recover from such an item is its scrap value".
  2. In oral evidence she has opined that Mr. Mitchell's percentage write-down amounts were generous and that we would be very lucky to get that amount. That evidence is just plain wrong and ought not be accepted by the Tribunal.
  3. Of the 4,132 Aged Stock items examined by Mr. Kosutic, in the 1997 and 1998 financial years, only 18 items were sold for less than retail cost.
  4. Retail cost is, of course, the Applicants' break-even point and is the wholesale acquisition cost together with the sales tax on the written down amount.
  5. Exhibit BK147 contains the analysis of all 18 Aged Stock items, which sold for less than retail cost. Thus, stock number 320318 was purchased on 14 December 1988 for a wholesale cost of $16.96 and was written down by 50% to a written down value of $8.48 (BK 147 line 1). The retail cost was $17.98 and the item was sold on 27 July 1996 for a sale price of $16.50 which is a loss on sale of $1.48.
  6. In the 1997 financial year 13 items of Aged Stock only, having an aggregate retail cost of $1,914.41 sold at retail for the aggregate amount of $1,860.00 which represents a total loss of $59.41 on those 13 items. Of that retail cost of $1,919.41 the amount of $1,461.71 is represented in one item stock number 010339 (line 12). In the 1998 financial year five items only were sold at less than retail cost and a loss of $22.38 was incurred.
  7. The evidence of Mr. Mitchell at paragraph 58 and Mrs. Ciprian's evidence at paragraphs 19, 20 and 42 ought not be believed. Eighteen items out of approximately four and a half thousand does not establish the Applicants' were "lucky" to get that amount. Quite the contrary. The Applicants would be extraordinarily "unlucky" to have to sell an item of Aged Stock below its retail cost.
  8. Mrs. Ciprian's evidence at paragraph 42 of her affidavit is set out above. For ease of reference this is what she said: "Often the item would be marked down to an extent greater than the discounted amount which appeared on the stock sheet; ie the retail 'sale' price would be less than that calculated by the program" (Emphasis added). Unlike the conclusion in Pontifex that statement does not stand a modicum of scrutiny, when over half of sales of Aged Stock items are for full ticket price and eighteen (18) items only sold for less than retail cost (economic cost), let alone less than the wholesale written down value. Paragraph 42 is, in that regard demonstrably false.
  9. Mrs. Ciprian in cross-examination, must be taken to have realized, and resiled from, the absurdity of what is written in paragraph 42. She was asked if the Applicants discounted at retail the same amount as at the wholesale level. Her answer speaks volumes "Not unless we absolutely had to otherwise the door would be shut" (TB page 218.9 to page 219.1) "Unless absolutely had to" is not "often".

Comparison of retail mark-up with wholesale percentage write down

  1. Exhibit BK106 (folio 3288) and exhibit 120 (folio 3399) are mirror image analyses in respect of the 1997 and 1998 years respectively. That analysis is directed towards testing another aspect of the so-called integral nexus argument advocated by Mr. Mitchell. That is to say it involves a comparison of the actual mark-up achieved on the retail sale as compared to the wholesale percentage write down amount as determined by Mr. Mitchell.
  2. At folio 3288 in respect of the 1997 financial year 1922 items of Aged Stock were sold in that year. 962 items, or approximately 50%, sold for full retail price. The retail cost (that is the economic cost or the break-even amount) of those 1922 Aged Stock items was $137,529.45. However, those 1922 items of Aged Stock actually sold for the amount of $292,162.74, a mark-up on retail cost of 112% (folio 3288).
  3. Put simply the average wholesale write-down percentage in respect of all 1922 Aged Stock items sold in the 1997 financial year was 72%. However, those 1922 items of Aged Stock sold for a mark-up of 112% on retail cost, which retail cost is in turn the aggregate of wholesale price plus sales tax of the written down value.
  4. The average wholesale write-down percentage in the 1998 financial year was 74% in respect of 2210 Aged Stock items sold in that year. However, those 2210 Aged Stock items had a retail cost (economic cost) of $124,977.82 but sold for the aggregate of $303,647.00, a mark-up of 143% (folio 3399).
  5. The analysis contained in results analysis five and ten (folios 3288 and 3399) points out in that analysis "the significant disparity between the postulated market selling value on the write down approach compared with the reality of what happened at retail level on sales of all stock over the year and the mark up that was achieved" (TC 349.19).

Restarting the clock and sales within the 12 month, 18 month and 36 month period

  1. Exhibit BK137 (folio 3504) contains an analysis of an Aged Stock item being written down immediately upon obtaining a defined milestone of 12 months, 18 months or 36 months age, irrespective of the selling pattern of that item.
  2. Notwithstanding that items of stock may have sold during the first 18 months after purchase "the clock doesn't start again and even though there have been sales of the very same item the maximum write down percentage is applied the moment you get to the 18 month" (TC 370.14).
  3. Thus, at BK137 (folio 3504) on 16 February 1995 six items of stock item number 281832 were purchased at a wholesale cost of $1.51. On 7 July 1996, that is, within the 18 month period, one item was sold for full ticket price of $19.95. The wholesale value of that item was $1.60 being wholesale cost plus 6%. Sales tax was 0.42 and the retail cost (economic cost) was $2.02.
  4. Notwithstanding the sale of one of the items within 18 months of its acquisition the remaining five items were written down by 90.1% on 16 August 1996.
  5. Accordingly, the written down value was $0.15, the sales tax on 15 cents was 5 cents and the retail cost (economic cost) was $1.56. On 2 October 1996 the second item from the original package was sold for full ticket price of $19.95. The remaining four items were sold in the course of the 1997 calender year.
  6. Thus, the actual sales record of an item was irrelevant to the question of writing down the wholesale value. Age, more specifically the milestones of 12 months, 18 months and 36 months was the sole criteria.

The repurchase of Aged Stock items

  1. At paragraph 58(h)(ii) Mr. Mitchell says that:

"an item of jewellery which is 18 months old will not, except in very exceptional circumstances, be of 'current model' because trends and fashion in jewellery change very quickly. Whether or not an item is a current model is considered in determining the valuation." (Emphasis added)

  1. In Exhibit BK97 (folio 3266) Mr. Kosutic examines whether Aged Stock items were repurchased or not. His methodology was as follows "We analysed every sale of Aged Stock over a two year period. Now, when an item of Aged Stock sold during a financial year we then went to the closing stock print for the end of that financial year to see if that same stock number was there and see if there was a new purchase date and to see if there was the same description as the items sold. Where the same stock number was there, where the description was the same and where there was a new purchase date we then entered that particular item into this spreadsheet, replacement Aged Stock 97 which is BK97." (TC page 371.13).
  2. The same analysis is undertaken in respect of the 1998 financial year at BK111 (folio 3379 and following).
  3. Thus, for example, on line 1 at folio 3266, Aged Stock item stock number 340311 was purchased on 25 November 1992 (column 3). It remained on hand for over three and a half years until it was sold on 9 July 1996 (column 4). The wholesale cost was $16.80 and it was written down by 90% to a written down value of $1.68. The item was sold for its full ticket price of $44.95 (column 8).
  4. However, notwithstanding Mr. Mitchell's assertion that Aged Stock is not current model and Mrs. Ciprian's oral evidence that they would be very "lucky" to sell Aged Stock items over three years of age for the written down value, this specific piece of stock was repurchased and replaced on 27 August 1996, that is, approximately six weeks after this three and a half year old piece of stock was sold. Precisely the same stock was purchased as a replacement at a price of $17.25.
  5. Exhibit BK97 identifies 22 Aged Stock items which have been replaced in this manner. In some instances (for example stock number 251697 at line 15) this replacement occurred within a matter of a couple of days after the sale of the Aged Stock. In respect of all 22 items the aggregate original wholesale cost was $1,618.13 which items were sold at retail for $3,958.20.
  6. More significantly, not only were the 22 items replaced they were replaced at a discount, that is, the wholesale cost of the replacement stock was only $1,487.30.
  7. In the 1998 financial year 60 such occurrences of the replacement and re-acquisition of Aged Stock items previously sold have been identified. The aggregate wholesale cost of those 60 items was $3,686.10 (folio 3381) and the written down value was $1,189.02. The 60 items were also replaced at a slight discount in the amount of $3,586.69.
  8. It is also the case that significant lag times were involved. Thus item one at folio 3266 was on hand for three years seven months and, according to Mr. Mitchell not of the current model and not readily saleable, yet it was replaced within six weeks.
  9. BK97 and BK111 establish that "notwithstanding that stock was written down, and showed that stock number by reason of alleged qualities or absence of qualities by reason of age, it is still deemed fit to be re-ordered . . . then sold profitably . . . [and] by and large repurchased from an external wholesaler at much the same price as the original stock, perhaps [and] at small discount, I think 2% or so". (TC page 373.22)
  10. At paragraph 58(h)(ii) Mr. Mitchell did add the qualification that "Except in very exceptional circumstances" the stock would not be the current model.
  11. In respect of these 82 items of Aged Stock sold in the 1997 and 1998 financial years, which Aged Stock were replaced with precisely the same stock after sale, not one iota of evidence has been led by the Applicants to suggest that these 82 particular Aged Stock items were, in any manner whatsoever, exceptional in the sense described by Mr. Mitchell.

Sale of Aged Stock during discount months

  1. The impression created by Mr. Mitchell in paragraph 58(f)(i) is that continuous efforts are required on the part of the jeweller to sell the Aged Stock, for example, "We assume the jeweller has taken steps to sell the stock, such as applying significant discounts and the like".
  2. At paragraph 20 of her affidavit Mrs. Ciprian conveys the same impression. Thus she talks of terms of an Aged Stock item "The chances of selling the item at its current, albeit already discounted, price is highly unlikely and such items will need to be even further discounted if they are to sell".
  3. At paragraph 42 of her affidavit Mrs. Ciprian says "Often the item would be marked down to an extent greater than the discounted amount which appeared on the stock sheet; ie the retail 'sale' price would be less than that calculated by the program".
  4. However, Exhibit BK122 (folio 3413) discloses regular instances where Aged Stock was sold for full ticket price, then discounted for sale during special sale months and, at the conclusion of the sale months, then sold at full ticket price for the remainder of the year (TC 374.13).
  5. Thus, Exhibit BK122 (folio 3413) discloses that eight individual items under stock number 161902 were acquired on 30 October 1995 at a wholesale cost of $16.58 and written down by 80% after 18 months to a written down value of $3.32. One item was sold on 2 January 1997 for full ticket price of $59.95. There was no retail discount.
  6. However, in the month of April 1997, being a sales month for the Albany store, three items were sold at retail at a 50% discount at the price of $29.95. Of course, the discounted retail sale price far exceeds the retail cost (or economic cost or break-even amount) being $17.64.
  7. The remaining three items on hand were then sold at full retail ticket price in the months after the conclusion of the sale in April 1997, in May 1997, December 1997 and January 1998.
  8. Thus, the impression created by the evidence of Mrs. Ciprian and Mr. Mitchell that Aged Stock has to be consistently and persistently discounted in order to achieve a sale is just wrong.
  9. Age is not a determinate. Aged Stock maybe sold on special during sales but it is sold again for the full retail ticket price after the conclusion of the sales month.

The sales results for the Katanning store

  1. The opening of the Katanning store in the course of the 1996 year of income provides a rare snapshot and an opportunity to test Mr. Mitchell's integral nexus hypotheses and that Aged Stock items need to be written down in value.
  2. The evidence establishes that Mr. Ciprian had a wealth of buying experience. Thus on the opening of the Katanning store a strategic decision was required. Should it be stocked with Aged Stock from the other stores or should new stock be acquired. It appears that overwhelmingly the stock in the Katanning store was acquired as new stock. This is established by BK 149, in that at 30 June 1996 the Katanning store had 4510 items on hand, of which only 202 or some 4.48% was Aged Stock. Since, at 30 June 1996 the Katanning store was less than 12 months old and, accordingly had not reached any of Mr. Mitchell milestone dates, all 202 of those items had to come from Aged Stock in the other stores.
  3. Given the Katanning store was predominately stocked with new stock, its opening presented with Mr. Ciprian with an opportunity to identify which items of stock were moving quickly and which items were likely to remain unsold after 12 months, 18 months and 36 months.
  4. Put bluntly, the opening of the Katanning store represented an opportunity to clear the decks and stock the shop with items that were not going to age, languish and depreciate in value.
  5. A right minded reasonable business person in Mr. Ciprain's position would, it is submitted, if Aged Stock did deteriorate in value in the manner postulated by Mr. Mitchell, take the opportunity to avoid having Aged Stock items on hand.
  6. However, by the end of the next financial year, that is 30 June 1997, the age profile of the Katanning store in respect of Aged Stock on hand is virtually indistinguishable from the Ciprian and Albany shops. Thus, at 30 June 1997 the Katanning store had 4,396 items on hand of which some 1,393 or approximately 32% was represented by Aged Stock on hand, whereas, the relevant percentage of Aged Stock on hand for Ciprian is 35% and 46% for Albany.
  7. The question then is how many of these 1,393 Aged Stock items in the Katanning store on 30 June 1997 were home grown Aged Stock and how many were transferred in. By looking at the acquisition date of the stock sheet for the Katanning store it is possible to identify those stock items which were purchased prior to the date of the opening of the Katanning store. There were only 146 such items out of the 1,363 Aged Stock items on hand in the Katanning store on 30 June 1997.
  8. Within the space of just one calender year the Katanning shop had, on its books as stock on hand both fast and slow moving stock, which implies, as a consequence, that the age of the stock does not necessarily mean that its value has diminished.
  9. The trading record of Katanning, as a new store, shows it is normal in the ordinary course of business to have both slow and fast moving stock in the jewellery industry.

Market selling value and written down value

  1. The fundamental point which emerges from the analysis of Mr. Kosutic appears at BK98 (folio 3271 in respect of the 1997 financial year and BK112 at folio 3385 in respect of the 1998 financial year).
  2. Thus, in respect of the 1997 financial year (folio 3271) 1898 items of Aged Stock were sold by the Applicants to Allay. Those 1898 items of Aged Stock were acquired at a cost of $124,329.81 (folio 3271 column 6). Yet, according to the wholesale percentage write downs advocated by Mr. Mitchell those 1898 items of Aged Stock should be written down in the books of Jade by 66% to the amount of $41,954.82.
  3. That written down value is said to be the "market selling value" of that stock on hand to the Applicants.
  4. Allay was able to sell, in the course of the 1997 financial year, those precise 1898 items of Aged Stock for an aggregate amount of $290,646.29. Allay is thus able to secure for itself a margin of $166,317 being the difference between $290,646 and $124,329. On an initial aggregate wholesale acquisition cost of $124,329 that profit margin of $166,317 (excluding sales tax) represents a mark-up of 134%.
  5. Yet the Applicants represent to the Commissioner that the market selling value to the Applicants trading as Jade in respect of those 1898 Aged Stock items is not their cost of $124,329 but rather an amount written down by 66%.
  6. Acquiring at cost, Allay secures for itself a profit of $166,317 being a margin of 134%.
  7. Yet the Applicants represent to the Commissioner that a reasonable but not too anxious vendor in the position of the Applicants would be prepared to sell its stock at a market selling value of $41,954.
  8. A reasonable not too anxious vendor would insist on at least recouping his cost and, in all probability, a margin on top, especially when, in turn, that purchaser is able to sell the stock at a mark-up above cost of 134%.
  9. The same analysis can be repeated in respect of the 1998 financial year: see BK 112 at folio 3385. Thus 2210 Aged Stock items having a wholesale cost of $115,446, with a ticketed price of $382,701 were sold for an aggregate amount of $303,647. That represents a mark up of $188,201 or 163%. But the Applicants represent to the Commissioner the "market selling value" is only $35,419.
  10. The suggested wholesale percentage write down and, as a consequence, the market selling value suggested by Mr. Mitchell is, with respect, arbitrary, capricious and perverse.

Penalties

  1. Additional tax was imposed under sec 226J of the 1936 Act at 75% and sec 226X(b)(i) being an additional 20% of the penalty under sec 226J, a total of 90% altogether (20% of 75% = 15% + 75% = 90%).
  2. By sec 226J penalty is imposed if the tax shortfall was caused by "intentional disregard by the taxpayer ... of this Act or the regulations".
  3. Sec 226X(b)(i) the penalty is imposed if "the taxpayer took steps to prevent or hinder the Commissioner from becoming aware of the shortfall"
  4. The Commissioner's contention as to what constitutes an intentional disregard of the Act or Regulations derives from paragraphs 21 and 22 of Taxation Ruling TR 94/4. Although not binding on the Tribunal it is open to the Tribunal to have regard to the Commissioner's guidelines. The relevant paragraphs are in the following terms:

In a tax context, penalty is attracted if a taxpayer intentionally disregards the ITAA or the regulations. Whether a taxpayer's disregard of the ITAA or regulations is intentional may be determined on the basis of direct evidence of the taxpayer's intention, but will more likely need to be inferred from the surrounding circumstances and conduct of the taxpayer. A taxpayer who does not include in his or her assessable income an amount of interest income may be suspected of having done so intentionally, but in the absence of an admission from the taxpayer that the omission was deliberate or conduct which might imply deliberate evasion, it would be difficult to judge that the taxpayer had intentionally excluded the amount from assessable income resulting in a 75% penalty being payable. On the other hand, if the interest omitted was from a bank account which the taxpayer had opened in a false name, this would be a circumstance which would infer that the taxpayer had acted intentionally.

23. It may be noted that for a taxpayer to intentionally disregard the ITAA or the regulations requires the taxpayer to know what the obligations under the ITAA or regulations are, and to choose to disregard them. Where, for example, the assessability of a particular amount is unclear, and a taxpayer chooses not to return the amount, the taxpayer would not have "disregarded" the ITAA, but would have taken a view of its effect which differs from the Commissioner's view. Provided that view was honestly held, and was not frivolous or unfounded, penalty for intentional disregard would not apply. The taxpayer may, of course, still be liable for penalty for lack of reasonable care or recklessness." (Emphasis added)

  1. Sec 31(1) of the 1936 Act allows a taxpayer to choose market selling value as the basis for accounting for an item of stock on hand. The Applicants assert that Mr. Mitchell's wholesale write-down percentages produce a proper measure of market selling value. In the Commissioner's submission, any view on the part of the taxpayer that the wholesale write-down percentages were an appropriate measure of market selling value cannot be honestly held, and is frivolous and or unfounded.
  2. The view that Mr. Mitchell's wholesale write-down percentages were an appropriate measure of market selling value is based upon the following propositions:

(a) There was an integral nexus between the discount offered at the retail level and what the amount of the discount at the wholesale level;

(b) That the value of stock deteriorated with age and that the Applicants were "lucky" to sell it for the written down value and that "often" it was discounted so that "the retail sale price would be less than that calculated by the program".

(c) That at 18 months Aged Stock should be valued as if it were being sold back to the wholesale merchant; and

(d) That at 36 months Aged Stock should be valued as if it were being sold for scrap.

  1. Propositions (a) and (b) have been fully dealt with in the submissions. To recap on one telling answer by Mrs. Ciprian, she was asked, did she discount at retail level as she did at wholesale and she relied "Not unless we absolutely had to otherwise the door would be shut".
  2. Market selling value as set out above was found to be the amount that could be realised in the taxpayers own selling market in the ordinary course of the taxpayer's business. Market selling value does not contemplate a forced sale.
  3. In relation to propositions (c) and (d) the write-downs for 18 to 36 months contemplate a sale at a hypothetical value in a hypothetical market. The written down value is based on the original cost of the item, which is then reduced by a pre-determined percentage that is solely based on time. The Applicants' methodology is, in effect, a form of depreciation. This value is not based on the Applicant's own selling market in the ordinary course of business.
  4. The write-down for stock aged 36 months and over contemplates a sale of stock to a refiner for the purpose of meltdown. For stones it contemplates a sale back to the stone merchant. This is not the Applicants' own selling market in the ordinary course of business. Stock aged over 36 months is never disposed of in this way. The Applicant has an option of using this method to dispose of its Aged Stock if it wishes, but chooses not to do so because it would result in too great a loss. This is not the Applicant's own selling market in the ordinary course of their business. Sales of trading stock by this means can reasonably be considered to be a forced sale. Valuation by this means is contrary to the decision in Australasian Jam.
  5. The Applicant's actions do not indicate a careful and cautious approach to their taxation affairs. Rather, their actions, objectively ascertained and scrutinized, establish a headlong and determined pursuit of a tax deduction to the exclusion of any modicum of reason or restraint.
  6. Thus, on 20 January 1993 Mrs. Ciprian wrote to Mr. Gibson as follows:

"Dear Peter, As advised for showcase it is possible to depreciate old stock for tax calcs. Can you please advise us of (i) the formula (if there is one!) (ii) the method?"

  1. In November 1993 Australian Sales Tax Consultants wrote to their clients, including the Applicants, and advised that the ATO "will not accept the sale of aged stock on or after 1 November 1993 at the revalued amount ... it remains our opinion that an independent valuation is the most appropriate mechanism to establish the true arm's length value of aged stock." (Emphasis added)
  2. The letter outlines three choices namely, continue to claim the wholesale percentage write down and deal with the consequences if and when an ATO audit occurs, comply with the tax office interpretation, or sell all aged stock to your retail entity before the ATO appointed deadline of 31 October 1993.
  3. The letter on its face cries out for a careful and cautious approach. A taxpayer in the position of the Applicants who know their own business and the selling patterns for Aged Stock should be put on notice, particularly by the words "an independent valuation" and a mechanism to establish "the true arm's length value of aged stock".
  4. The Applicants knew, or must be taken to have known, at all times the true arm's length value of aged stock and that it did not deteriorate in value merely by age.
  5. In November 1993 Australian Sales Tax Consultants obtained an opinion from Mr. A.H Slater QC. On 11 February 1994 (Exhibit DC07, page 37 Affidavit of Mrs. Ciprian) Australian Sales Tax Consultants wrote to the Applicants and advised of the existence of Mr. Slater's opinion that "the taxable value of sales of aged stock between associated entities need not exceed a price which a retailer dealing at arm's length would be prepared to pay for the goods."
  6. However, the letter contained an odd twist which did not pass unnoticed by Mrs. Ciprian, namely, that "We are prepared to supply the benefit of Queens Counsel's opinion. We are prepared to show you the opinion, however, we are not in a position to supply you with a copy of same."
  7. Mrs. Ciprian has rightly underlined the words "we are not in a position to supply you with a copy of same" and placed two question marks against those words in the margins.
  8. As a matter of logistics " showing" the opinion means either the Applicants travelling from Albany to Sydney or vice versa.
  9. The opinion by Mr. Slater is in evidence as Exhibit PG07 to the Affidavit of Mr. Gibson. It is worth noting, in full, the caveat to the opinion as follows:

"I add the cautionary note that the foregoing advice is predicated upon the value adopted being an arm's length value. The circumstance that an independent valuer, having no business connection with the wholesaler and retailer other than his engagement to provide a valuation, and having appropriate levels of expertise and experience, provides a valuation of the jewellery for the purpose of fixing the transfer price, is potent evidence that the price adopted is an arm's length price and it may, of course, be the fact that the valuer is simply wrong; in a dispute, a Court or Tribunal might adopt in preference a higher value attributed to the goods by a valuer called by the Commissioner. As the many cases in which competing valuations have been assessed by Courts and Tribunals indicate, independent valuers acting in good faith may reach different figures. However, if the valuer selected is accepted by the Taxation Office as being indeed and independent valuer, I should not expect there to be any contest over the figures adopted by him.

In this regard, I would point out that the consistent use by taxpayers (especially a group of taxpayers having a common advisor) of but one valuer may both lead the Taxation Office to question his independence, and indeed imperil that independence: the prospect of continued work (and revenue) may afford an incentive to make valuations which are more rather than less palatable to the client. A panel of independent valuers, amongst whom the work is spread, is likely to be both more reliable and more acceptable."

  1. Mr. Slater's caveat could not be more direct, pointed and telling. There is, of course, no evidence that either any of the Applicants or their advisor, Mr. Harrison, actually read the Slater opinion. But nonetheless, it was, to the Applicants knowledge available on request.
  2. On 3 February 1994 Mr. Gibson obtained advice from Borough & Partners (Exhibit DC20 page 75). This written advice was received by the Applicants. However, that advice in turn is subject to three specific caveats which set out in paragraphs five and six of the letter as follows:

"It should also be mentioned that the income tax concept of 'market selling value' has been considered upon a number of occasions by the Courts and has developed a relatively clear meaning. For example, it refers to the market selling value of the stock in the taxpayer's own selling market and not some other market. The Commissioner of Taxation also takes the view that selling expenses – i.e. costs which may be incurred in the future in effecting a sale of the goods do not come into the valuation of market selling value. You will appreciate from these comments that the Commissioner of Taxation might take issue with the amount ascribed to stock by an independent valuer upon the basis it does not reflect 'market selling value' in an income tax context.

...

It follows, therefore, that the adoption of the independent valuations proposed would not, subject only the above stated matters, be disputed by the Commissioner of Taxation. (We note again for completeness that the goods in question represent only certain stock lines and thus their sale at a loss should not result in an overall loss for taxation purposes being incurred by the wholesale entities. In the event such a loss were to be incurred, we understand you are aware of the various means by which this loss might be utilized)." (Emphasis in the original)

  1. The Borough's advice is expressly qualified by reference to the sale in the taxpayers own selling market and not some other market, for example, a fire sale or an export market. It is also predicated on some specific items only being discounted and that no loss is disclosed at the wholesale level.
  2. The Mitchell valuation is, of course, based upon a sale in markets that the Applicants do not trade in, namely, a sale back to wholesalers and a sale for scrap. The Applicants knew, or must be taken to have known, that they did not sell in those markets and that they could not satisfy the caveat. This is particularly so, given the "not" was emphasized in the Borough's letter i.e. "and not some other market".
  3. Mr. Harrison understood the expression taxpayers own selling market to mean the wholesale selling market in which the Applicants operated (TB page 152.7).
  4. Mr. Harrison also agreed that if 37 out of 40 stock items were reduced then perhaps "that doesn't tie in with that spirit" of the Borough's advise that the goods represented "only certain stock lines" (TB 154.2).
  5. Mr. Harrison also understood that Borough's assumed that the wholesale percentage write-down would not result in an overall loss for taxation purposes being incurred at the wholesale level (TB 154.2). Mr Harrison was aware that the occurrence of such a loss at the wholesale level was precisely what happened in the Applicants' case (TB 154.3).
  6. Accordingly, Mr. Harrison was actually aware of, and understood the implications of the three caveats in the Borough's advice, namely, the taxpayer's own market, certain items only written down, and there be no tax loss at the wholesale level.
  7. On 21 November 1994 Australian Sales Tax Consultants wrote their letter entitled "IT'S TIME! STOCK REVALUATION".
  8. The first paragraph of the letter referred to the previous valuation on 5 February 1993 and continued as follows "As it has been some time you should now repeat the exercise. Similarly, if you stopped revaluing stock late last year you should consider restarting as there are considerable advantages". Those advantages included, for example, that watches were now being written down at 12 months of age and stock over three years old was now being reduced by up to 90%.
  9. Going on from the receipt of that letter of 21 November 1994 the Applicants have made a conscious decision to proceed with a further stock revaluation to secure "the considerable advantages" and such stock assessment was received on 23 June 1995 (DC10 page 41).
  10. Not only did the Applicants proceed with the further stock revaluation but, in the implementation, of that further stock revaluation the Applicants accelerated to the maximum write down at the earliest date. The accelerated write down is explained above at paragraphs 296 and following.
  11. Objectively viewed the Applicants, conduct can be described as the headlong pursuit of the tax deduction irrespective of their own knowledge of the sales performance of their Aged Stock and their own knowledge that they did not sell back to their wholesale suppliers or, much less, sell their Aged Stock into a scrap market. Further, their accountant was aware of the caveats contained in the Borough's advise and that the Applicants fell squarely within each of the caveats.
  12. The Applicants' conduct in claiming the deduction shows an intentional calculated disregard of what were the proper requirements of a market selling value under section 31(1) of the 1936 Act.
  13. The Applicants' purported view that the wholesale write-down percentages arrived at by Mr. Mitchell were appropriate valuations is, in all the circumstances, frivolous or unfounded within Taxation Ruling 94/4 paragraph 23.
  14. Accordingly, additional tax by way of penalty is properly imposed under section 226J of the 1936 Act.
  15. By section 226X further additional tax applies if the taxpayer took steps to prevent or hinder the Commissioner from becoming aware of the tax shortfall. In this context hinder means to obstruct or to obscure.
  16. On 29 January 1998 (DC15 page 52) the Commissioner advised that the practice of devaluing Aged Stock generally held within an interposed wholesale company prior to sale to the associated retail company is not acceptable to the Commissioner and not in accordance with subsection 31(1) of the 1936 Act. The letter advised:

"This office does not consider that the written down value of the stock on hand in the wholesale jewellers' books of account satisfied the above provisions. Therefore, if you have used the written down value for the calculation of your income, you are required to review your valuation for stock on hand as at 30 June 1996. Should you find that there has been an error or omission made in your calculation, you are requested to advise this office in writing by 27 February 1998. The disclosure of any errors or omissions should include the name and file number under which your business lodges a taxation return, and the amount of any adjustment(s) required. Also, please include a brief explanation of why the error was made."

  1. The letter concluded with a note that voluntary disclosures made at this time would result in the reduction of penalties to a rate significantly lower than would otherwise be imposed.
  2. That letter produced a response from the Applicants on 20 February 1998, which reads in full as follows:

"Re: your letter of 29th Jan 1998. I am satisfied that no error nor omission was made in our calculation of income with regard to our stock on hand as at 30th June 1996."

  1. As a matter of fact the letter of 20th February 1998 (DC16 page 59) did obstruct the Commissioner and cause the Commissioner to form the view, at least initially, that the Applicants were not using wholesale percentage write downs at all (TC 387.1, 387.10 and 387.34).
  2. Under cross-examination Mrs Ciprian was asked: "So you knew full well as a result of receiving that letter of 29 January 1998 that you were not permitted on the Commissioner's view of things to continue to use the stock write down system?". Mrs Ciprian replied: "I knew that the Commissioner did not approve of that system" (TB 263.1).
  3. The response by the Applicants was accepted by Mr. Kosutic and he was, in that respect, actually misled by the reply (TC 390.29).
  4. Mr. Kosutic then noticed something that he thought was odd in that he received a number of replies which were worded in sort of or in a similar or identical manner to that of the Applicants (TC page 387.17). The Applicants' case was picked at random and selected for audit to ascertain what the reply actually meant (TC page 387.19).
  5. In the circumstances the Commissioner was, as a fact, misled, obstructed, delayed and hindered from ascertaining the correct position regarding the Applicants' use of wholesale percentage write downs.
  6. The origin of the Applicants' letter of 20 February 1998 is somewhat obscure. Mr. Harrison has no recall of drafting or assisting in the drafting of the letter (TB 148.5 to TB 148.9).
  7. Mr. Gibson in cross-examination conceded that he may have suggested to Mrs. Ciprian that she could respond to the ATO letter in the manner that she did (TB 486.23). In re-examination Mr. Gibson has said he has no recollection of actually assisting Mrs. Ciprian and that he was unlikely that he did so assist, because he didn't advise in relation to income tax matters (TB page 488.20).
  8. Accordingly, no professional adviser has any recollection of assisting in the preparation of the reply.
  9. Mr. Harrison in his evidence understood that the Commissioner's letter of 29 January 1998 signalled a serious change in attitude on the part of the Commissioner (TB page 140.7). Mr. Harrison clearly understood, based on 15 years experience as a chartered accountant, that if a voluntary disclosure was made in response, penalties would be reduced below the level otherwise imposed (TB page 140.9). In a nutshell Mr. Harrison knew and understood that in January 1998 "the Commissioner didn't think that it [the write-down methodology] was 'okay'" (TB page 141.6).
  10. Mr. Harrison has no recall of any professional input into the reply of 20th February 1998. He agreed that as a careful, cautious and prudent chartered accountant he would not have responded to the Commissioner in the terms of Mrs. Ciprian's letter (TB page 149.3 to 149.5).
  11. Mr. Harrison agreed that a much more suitable response to the Commissioner's letter would have been in terms of "Receipt is acknowledged of your letter, my client disagrees with your assertion that the practice is unacceptable, nonetheless without admission here are the relevant figures that you have requested. My clients' rights to object are expressly preserved and I seek the Commissioner's confirmation that my client will qualify for the penalty remission" (TB pages 149.5 to 149.7). Mr Harrison agreed that that would have been "one possible response" and would have been "a suitable response".
  12. Mr. Harrison also agreed that Mrs. Cirprian's letter dated 7 April 1998 (DC18 page 63) which included the words "Please find enclosed a supplementary return which we are willing to lodge on a without prejudice basis. We do not admit any liability and reserve the right to request a refund in the event that the technical issue is favourably resolved" was an altogether more careful and cautious response than the letter of 20 February 1998 (TB 150.3).
  13. The letter of 20 February 1998 was startling in its coyness and was an exercise in excessive, pedantic literalism.
  14. That is, the Applicant's assert they properly responded to the Commissioner, and answered precisely what the Commissioner had asked: that is, we have checked the arithmetic accuracy of our returns and there is no mathematical error or omission made in the calculation of our income regarding our stock in trade.
  15. Objectively viewed, the letter was an exercise in obfuscation, and, as a fact, hindered and delayed the Commissioner from ascertaining the true position. Section 226X(b)(i) is satisfied in that additional tax equal to an additional 20% of the section 226J penalty is properly imposed.

Dated: 23 July 2002



David McGovern



Ian Young



Counsel for the Respondent

COMMISSIONER OF TAXATION

OF THE COMMONWEALTH OF AUSTRALIA



ATS



CIPRIAN



RESPONDENT'S SUPPLEMENTARY SUBMISSIONS



  1. At paragraphs 281 and following of the Commissioner's original submissions it is contented that the evidence of Mr Kosutic was both relevant and admissible. This was put on the basis that, for example, Mr Kosutic had undertaken an inspection, identification and sorting of primary evidentiary material that was properly before the Tribunal and was, in any event the Applicants' own documentation.

  2. Specific support for the admissibility of Mr Kosutic's evidence is provided by the decision in Potts v Miller [1940] HCA 43; (1940) 64 CLR 282 at pages 302.7 to 303.9 per Dixon J who relied upon the earlier decision in Myer v Sefton [1817] EngR 221; (1817) 171 E.R. 644. In that case evidence was proposed to be adduced from a witness who had examined the books and accounts of a bankrupt and, on the basis of that inspection, the witness was to give evidence of what the value of the property in question was.

  3. At page 302.9 Dixon J said "From the very nature of the case, such an enquiry could not be made in Court, and therefore evidence on such a point must be given by someone who had had the means of enquiry, and who could state the result. With respect to the source from which the knowledge of the witness was drawn, in the present instant, a commission of bankrupt had issued, and the documents from which the result was obtained, had been rendered by the bankrupt."

  4. Similarly, in this case, the sort of enquiry and analysis undertaken by Mr. Kosutic, though available to the Tribunal if it had the hearing time, could not realistically be made by the Tribunal itself and, evidence was admissible from an appropriate person who had the means of enquiry and, could state the result, and, the primary source material was identified.

  5. Dixon J continued and noted that cases had arisen from time to time where under special circumstances accounts between master and servant, tradesman and shopkeeper, and banker and customer had been admitted as evidence in the cause. His Honour continued "When such an occasion arises and the books are allowed in evidence or their production is not insisted upon, an accountant's statement of the result of his examination is receivable as the evidence of a person of skill (citation omitted). Little English authority will be found explaining the grounds upon which the books of account kept according to an established system in organised business are receivable in evidence as proof, not of the occurrence some particular fact recorded or indicated by a specific entry or narration, but of the financial progress or result of business operations conducted on a large scale. Common sense has prevailed and such materials are used in practice without objection."

  6. The Applicants assert in paragraphs 3 and 4 of their addendum that "ageing" affects the value of such stock and that Aged Stock in the mass produced jewellery industry is difficult to sell. However, the analysis by Mr. Kosutic examined all Aged Stock items sold in the 1997 and 1998 financial years. That analysis discloses one indisputable fact, namely, that approximately 50% of all Aged Stock items sold for full retail ticket price and only 18 items out of approximately 4,200 Aged Stock sold for less than retail cost.

  7. The Applicants criticised Mr Kosutic for reliance upon photocopies and that there were two missing pages in his version of the stock reports. So much is readily conceded but, with respect, the criticism is nitpicking and de minimis. Contrary to paragraph 14(iii) of the addendum Mr Kosutic did seek an explanation from the Applicants as to the meaning of their stock prints: see paragraphs 49 to 102 of Mr. Kosutic's statement of 10 January 2001.

  8. Some implied criticism of Mr Kosutic is raised by the Applicants in that he was not aware that Aged Stock was sold by Jade to Allay at the written down value only (addendum paragraph 14(iii)). Again, with respect, the sales prints for Jade to Allay were not available, were not produced to Mr Kosutic, and were not produced to this Tribunal notwithstanding a formal call for such documents: see Commissioner's submissions (at paragraph 293).

  9. The Applicants (addendum paragraph 14(v)) point to entries recording a retail cost of one cent and the sale of stock that did not appear to be on hand at 30 June 1996. The question of stock items not on hand is dealt with at paragraphs 354 and 355 of the Commissioner's submissions. In respect of both items the Applicants have not produced one shred of evidence to suggest the effect is other than minimal.

  10. The Applicants at paragraph 14(vi) point to service costs for fitting watch bands and resizing rings. But again, the Applicants have not adduced one iota of evidence to establish that these suggested costs of fitting a watch band and resizing a ring were anything other than peppercorn in nature. More so given that they have had the benefit of Mr Kosutic's analysis since January 2001.

  11. The Applicants are critical that no account is taken of those items that were either not written down or, alternatively, the subject of a full 100% write down. In simple terms a zero percent write down meant that Mr Mitchell did not discount those items, for example, pearls. On the other hand a 100% write down shows the items were, in fact, given away usually under the description of miscellaneous gift. In any event, such gifts were again, insignificant in nature. There were 11 such gifts in 1997 (folio 3268-3269) and 13 in 1998 (see folio 3382-3383).

  12. Most significantly the Applicants assert in paragraph 14(ix) that only 19% of Aged Stock is sold at full ticket price and that 81% is sold at a discount. However, that assertion is wrong.

  13. The evidence relied upon is the mere assertion of Mr Gibson. Mr Kosutic analysed all sales of Aged Stock. Self serving assertion cannot prevail over clinical exhaustive logic.

  14. Moreover, in cross examination Mr Gibson was asked "You don't have any material that you bring forth to challenge the accuracy of the material though, do you?" Mr Gibson's answer speaks volumes: "I don't have any material. No I don't have anything that challenges the accuracy of what he did". (TB page 487.24)

  15. The expert evidence of Mr. McClintock was adduced at a time and on the assumption that primary reliance would be placed by the Applicants upon the evidence of Mr Mitchell. In view of the case presented by the Applicants in closing address much of the expert evidence relating to the preparation of accounts and accounting standards is now of lesser significance.

  16. However, as Mr McClintock's evidence makes clear, he brings to the Tribunal a wealth of practical experience of preparing accounts and, in particular, whether the accounts are "true and fair and the directors or others [can] sign off as well, as being as true and fair" (TC 517.28).

  17. As Mr McClintock has explained the accountant's concept of "net realisable value" is related to the tax concept of "market selling value" in section 31(1). That is to say net realisable value is a net concept and selling expenses such as commissions, freight costs, transport costs are subtracted in the calculation of net realisable value but not for the purposes of ascertaining a market value or a market selling value (TC 520.16). Accordingly, market selling value is likely to be higher than net realisable value (TC 520.25).

  18. Accordingly, the process by which Mr McClintock ascertains net realisable value has some relevance to the ascertainment of a market selling value for the purposes of section 31(1).

  19. What the evidence of Mr McClintock makes clear is that, for the purposes of presenting the accounts so as to show a true and fair view of the stock on hand, whilst in the first year a report from an expert as to the market selling value of stock on hand might be accepted without question, in the second year, Mr McClintock would consider "Whether or not the expert had made any assumptions in valuation and consider whether or not that expert could be normally expected to come up with the right answer." (TC 521.5).

  20. The problem that Mr McClintock would be confronted with in the second year is that he would have evidence that Allay has been selling the stock at considerably higher values than it has been buying the Aged Stock at, and having regard to the relationship between Allay and Jade, that Allay's selling value is considerably higher than the market value given to the Aged Stock by Jade (TC 521.10).

  21. More particularly "Jade is selling its stock at such a low value to Allay on the basis of the valuations that have been prepared and that it could make more money by selling it to other retailers or to other markets and that would leave me as an auditor to question whether or not the valuation was actually correct and notwithstanding I'm not an expert in jewellery valuation it's a common sense questioning." (TC 522.5)

  22. Mr McClintock gave evidence concerning the very situation raised in submissions of a sale by Jade to Allay at a token peppercorn $1 and that Jade's market was Allay and Allay alone. Mr McClintock replied as follows: "I was just going to say in that context, deputy president, you could argue that the market is simply Allay and that's the market price but where would that end? I mean does that mean if Allay is prepared to offer a $1 then that's the market value under section 31? Shouldn't it be the market value if Jade was selling to Allay or into the market place selling to Allay or other retailers. That would in my mind constitute what I would look at in considering net realisable value for the purposes of the accounts." (TC 522.25)

  23. In Mr McClintock's view in arriving at the net realisable value (which gives a true and fair view) by the deduction of any selling costs from market selling value for tax purposes, he would have regard to "the ultimate margin that was realised by Allay" (TC 523.10).

  24. This is because Allay is earning a super normal profit, that is Jade bears the disproportionate risk of obsolescence and Jade is precluded from charging to Allay a proper market price and Jade has as a consequence a history of losses and is actually losing money in selling its stock to Allay (TC 529.30-TC530.19).

  25. Inevitably Jade has, as a consequence of such losses, reached the point of having a net deficiency of assets and cannot continue without continued financial support by the Applicants or Allay. Contrary to the position in Pontifex 2000 ATC 4642 at page 4,647 paragraph 13, in this case there is actual evidence of Jade operating at a substantial loss and ultimately, having a net deficiency of assets.

  26. In Mr McClintock's opinion a market value "cannot be determined in the context of trade between two related parties where one party is unfairly treated by the other" (TC 527.17). A true market value, or a market selling value for tax purposes, would need to be considered in the context of a wider market "because if Jade had a mind of its own why would it sell to Allay such that Allay earns the higher margins and why wouldn't it sell to a third party where that third party would be accepting lower margins" (TC 527.20).

  27. In other words, a true and fair view of Jade's market selling value would be the value it could be sold to another retailer who did not abrogate to itself the right to earn super normal profits at Jade's expense.

  28. Mr. McClintock's evidence is compelling. In practical terms his evidence speaks volumes. Professor Walker, on the other hand, had no practical appreciation of Jade's operations, he had, for example, not availed himself of the opportunity of reading Jade and Allay's accounts, was not aware that Jade bore no costs and most significantly, not aware that Jade was in a loss position. His evidence cannot be preferred to Mr. McClintock in that regard.

David McGovern



Ian Young



Counsel for the Respondent



SUMMARY OF RESULTS ANALYSIS



CIP/ALB/KAT AGED SALES 97 & 98



  • This is a full 100% list of every aged stock item that sold over a two year period. This is not a sample; it is an objective list rather than a valuation that is subjective.

AGED SALES NO STOCK RECORD 97 & 98 - ALL STORES



  • 97 Year. This is a list of items that were purchased before 30 June 1996 that were sold after 30 June 1996 but did not appear on the list for closing stock as at 30 June 1996.
  • Same applies to 98 year.
  • There appears to be a body of stock that is not recorded in the closing stock prints.
  • Because we've only detected the items that sold there are probably other items still on hand which are not included in the total closing stock.
  • Most of this stock appears to be purchased in the late 1980's.
  • This seems to show that closing stock has been understated by approximately $3,000 in 1997 and $4,000 in 1998.

REPLACEMENT AGED STOCK 97 & 98 - ALL STORES



  • This is a list of aged stock that sold and then replaced.
  • This is not a complete list because of the limited information we worked from. For an item to appear on this list it needed to be aged when sold, then needed to be replaced after sale and then remain unsold before the end of the financial year.
  • In 1997 stock costing $1,618.13 was replaced for $1,487.30. This stock was actually sold for $3,958.20. Of the 22 items that were replaced 14 cost equal to or greater than the original wholesale cost price.
  • In 1998 stock costing $3,686.10 was replaced for $3,586.69. This stock was actually sold for $10,056.70. Of the 60 items that were replaced 37 cost equal to or greater than the original wholesale cost price.

RESULTS ANALYSIS 1



  • This analysis shows:

(a) the number and percentages of items that sold for full retail price

(b) compares the average retail discount percentage to the wholesale writedown percentage

(c) it also compares the wholesale writedown percentage calculated by using dollar values to the retail discount percentage allowed calculated by using dollar values.

  • Of the 1922 items sold 962 sold for the full retail price with 960 selling for some retail discount. This is over 50% of aged stock sold for the full price in the 1997 year.
